\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\mathbbm{1}\left\lparen\mathcal{E}_{h,q}([Bm_{\mathrm{crowd}},\infty)\right\rparen\leqslant\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}\frac{\varepsilon}{h_{\max}}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

with probability at least
1-4\delta
.

We thus conclude by a union bound over the above three events.
∎

Lemma 3.23

(Tiny weight classes)
.

Let
q>q_{\max}
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
, it holds for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that

\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

Proof.

For the weight classes
q>q_{\max}
, the total leverage score mass contribution is bounded by

\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lVert W_{q}(\bm{\lambda})\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{q>q_{\max}}2^{1-q}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\leqslant\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{dnh_{\max}}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\leqslant\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{dh_{\max}}.

Then in expectation, the sum of the scaled leverage score samples (Definition
3.14
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\E\left\lparen\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\mathcal{S}_{h,q}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}_{i}\in W_{q}}\E\left\lparen\frac{1}{p_{h}}b_{i,h}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lVert W_{q}(\bm{\lambda})\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{dh_{\max}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta\varepsilon}{d}.

Then with probability at least
1-\delta
, the above sum is at most
\varepsilon/d
. We condition on this event. Then, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}_{i}\in W_{q}}\frac{1}{p_{h}}b_{i,h}(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leqslant d\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}_{i}\in W_{q}}\frac{1}{p_{h}}b_{i,h}\bm{\lambda}_{i}

Lemma
3.18

\displaystyle\leqslant d\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\frac{\varepsilon}{d}=\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

as desired.
∎

Lemma 3.24
.

There is an event with probability
1-11\delta
such that conditioned on this event, for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant(1+8\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\frac{2q_{\max}}{\alpha}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.23
, the contribution from weight classes
q>q_{\max}
is at most
\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}
with probability at least
1-\delta
. We let this event be
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and restrict our attention to
q\leqslant q_{\max}
.

For each
q\in[q_{\max}]
, we bound the mass contribution of rows corresponding to
W_{q}(\bm{\lambda})
at each subsampling level
\{0\}\cup[h_{\max}]
. Note that by Lemma
3.22
, there is an event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
with probability at least
1-6\delta
such that all levels
h,q
except for those such that
h=0
or
p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\in[m_{\mathrm{crowd}},Bm_{\mathrm{crowd}})
are bounded by at most
5\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}
, so it remains to bound these levels. These are the
0
th level of subsampling (i.e., no subsampling) and the Goldilocks levels.

Note that there exists at most one Goldilocks level
h\in[h_{\max}]
such that
p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\in[m_{\mathrm{crowd}},Bm_{\mathrm{crowd}})
. In this case, Lemma
3.20
applies since
m_{\mathrm{crowd}}\geqslant 3d^{2}\varepsilon^{-1}\log\alpha
, and we have that

\lVert\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\sum_{\bm{\lambda}_{i}\in W_{q}(\bm{\lambda})}\frac{\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rvert}{p_{h}}b_{i,h}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+4\varepsilon\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

with probability at least
1-2/\alpha
. If such a Goldilocks subsampling level
h
exists, then note that

p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\geqslant m_{\mathrm{crowd}}\implies\lvert W_{q}\rvert\geqslant B^{u+h-1}m_{\mathrm{crowd}}\geqslant B^{u}m_{\mathrm{crowd}}\geqslant bN_{0}.

Then by Lemma
3.21
, the
0
th level of subsampling level contributes mass at most
(\varepsilon/h_{\max})\lVert W_{q}(\bm{\lambda})\rVert_{1}
with probability at least
1-4\delta/h_{\max}q_{\max}
. Thus by a union bound over all
q
s with a Goldilocks level and summing over these, the
0
th level contributes at most

\sum_{q\in q_{\max}}\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(0}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\mathbbm{1}(\exists h:p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\in[m_{\mathrm{crowd}},Bm_{\mathrm{crowd}}))\leqslant\sum_{q\in q_{\max}}\frac{\varepsilon}{h_{\max}}\lVert W_{q}(\bm{\lambda})\rVert_{1}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

Let this be event
\mathcal{E}_{3}
. On the other hand, for the Goldilocks level itself, there is a
1-2q_{\max}/\alpha
probability that

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert\mathbbm{1}\left\lparen\mathcal{E}_{h,q}([m_{\mathrm{crowd}},Bm_{\mathrm{crowd}}))\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leqslant\;

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}(1+\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\mathbbm{1}(\exists h:p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\in[m_{\mathrm{crowd}},Bm_{\mathrm{crowd}}))+4\varepsilon\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\;

\displaystyle 4\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}(1+\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\mathbbm{1}(\exists h:p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\in[m_{\mathrm{crowd}},Bm_{\mathrm{crowd}}))

by a union bound over the at most
q_{\max}
weight classes.

Otherwise, if a weight class
q
has no Goldilocks level, then we have by the triangle inequality that

\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(0}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

and thus we simply bound the contribution of the
0
th level by
\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}
.

Note that
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{3}
occurs with probability at least
1-11\delta
. Then conditioned on this event, every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has a
1-2q_{\max}/\alpha
probability that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\right]+\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}\left[\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(0}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}(\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+\underbrace{(1+\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}_{\text{Goldilocks or $0$th level}}+\underbrace{\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}_{\text{$0$th level if Goldilocks level exists}}+\underbrace{5\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}_{\text{badly concentrated and oversampled levels}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+8\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

which is the desired bound.
∎

We conclude by a standard net argument.

Theorem 3.25

(No expansion)
.

With probability at least
1-11\delta
, we have that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant(1+11\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.24
, there is an event with probability at least
1-10\delta
such that conditioned on this event, for each
\mathbf{x}
, there is a
1-2/\alpha
probability that

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant(1+8\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

(1)

It is well-known (see e.g.,
[
BLM+89
]
), that there exists an
\varepsilon
-net
\mathcal{N}
of size at most
(3/\varepsilon)^{d}=\exp(d\log(3/\varepsilon))
over the set
\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert=1\right\}
. Then by a union bound over the net, Equation
1
holds for every
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{N}
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Finally, let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be arbitrary with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=1
. It is shown in
[
WW19
, Theorem 3.5]
that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{y}^{(i)}
where each nonzero
\mathbf{y}^{(i)}
has
\mathbf{y}^{(i)}/\lVert\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\rVert_{1}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\lVert\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\varepsilon^{i}
. We then have that

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant(1+8\varepsilon)\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant(1+8\varepsilon)\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{i}\leqslant 1+11\varepsilon.

We conclude by homogeneity.
∎

4
Near Optimal Trade-offs for
\ell_{1}
Entrywise Embeddings

In this section, we obtain algorithmic trade-offs between sketching dimension and distortion for
\ell_{1}
entrywise embeddings, and show that this is nearly tight for
d\times d
matrices.

4.1
Algorithm

Our algorithm is an
M
-sketch with subsampling rates
p_{h}=B^{-h}
, where
B=(\frac{d}{\delta}\log n)^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
CountSketch
hashes into
\tilde{\Theta}(\frac{B}{\delta}\log n)
buckets. By homogeneity, we assume that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}=1
throughout this section.

Definition 4.1

(Useful constants)
.

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\lparen\frac{d}{\delta}\log n\right\rparen^{\alpha}

\displaystyle h_{\max}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log_{B}n

\displaystyle q_{\max}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log_{2}\frac{ndh_{\max}}{\delta}

\displaystyle p_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq B^{-h},

\displaystyle h\in[h_{\max}]

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Then there exists a sparse oblivious
\ell_{1}
entrywise embedding
\mathbf{S}
into
k
dimensions with

k=\left\lparen\frac{d}{\delta}\log n\right\rparen^{\alpha}\poly(\delta^{-1},\log n)

such that for any
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,

\ProbOp\left\{\Omega(1)\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\delta\alpha}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\right\}\geqslant 1-\delta.

Our analysis revolves around the vector of row norms.

Definition 4.3

(Row norms vector)
.

For an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{A}
with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}=1
, we define the row norms vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
\mathbf{a}_{i}=\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}
. Using this vector, we define weight classes
W_{q}(\mathbf{a})
and restrictions
\mathbf{A}^{(q)}
of
\mathbf{A}
to our weight classes, analogously to the analysis in Section
3
.

In order to avoid shrinking the vector
\mathbf{a}
by more than a constant factor with probability at least
\delta
, we apply Lemma
B.1
with failure rate
\delta
and constant
\varepsilon
, which gives an
M
-sketch with
0
th level hash bucket size

N_{0}=\tilde{O}\left\lparen\frac{B}{\delta}\log\log n\right\rparen

and
h
th level hash bucket size

N=\tilde{O}\left\lparen B\log n\right\rparen.

We now show that this does not dilate the entrywise
1
-norm of
\mathbf{A}
by more than
O(1/\alpha)
. As in the analysis in
[
VZ12
]
, we use the Rademacher dimension.

Lemma 4.4

(Rademacher dimension of
\ell_{1}^{d}
)
.

Let
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{s}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{x}_{i}\rVert_{1}\leqslant 1
for each
i\in[s]
, and let
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
\{\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{s}
be independent Rademacher variables. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{s}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant d\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2d}{\delta}}\sqrt{s}.

Proof.

The proof uses standard concentration inequalities and is similar to
[
VZ12
, Lemma 1]
. The details are deferred to Appendix
C
.
∎

We follow the approach of
[
VZ12
]
. Using the Rademacher dimension, we first show that if we sample too many elements, then the contribution from this level is at most a negligible fraction of the total mass.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
q\in[q_{\max}]
. Let
p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert\geqslant bN
for

b=2d^{2}h_{\max}^{2}q_{\max}^{2}\log\left\lparen\frac{2dNh_{\max}q_{\max}}{\delta}\right\rparen

Then with probability at least
1-\delta/q_{\max}
,

\sum_{h:p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert\geqslant bN}\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{1}{q_{\max}}\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}

Proof.

By Chernoff bounds, the probability that we sample
(1\pm 1/2)p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert/2N
elements in a given bucket in the
h
th level is at most

\exp\left\lparen-\frac{(1/2)^{2}p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert/N}{3}\right\rparen\leqslant\exp\left\lparen-\frac{b}{12}\right\rparen\leqslant\exp\left\lparen-\log\left\lparen\frac{Nh_{\max}q_{\max}}{\delta}\right\rparen\right\rparen=\frac{\delta}{Nh_{\max}q_{\max}}

so by a union bound over the
N
buckets, this holds simultaneously for all buckets at the
h
th level with probability at least
\delta/h_{\max}q_{\max}
.

We condition on the above event. Then, each bucket
L
is a randomly signed sum of
s\geqslant b
elements
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
with
\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2^{1-q}
. Thus by Lemma
4.4
, with probability at least
\delta/Nh_{\max}q_{\max}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{\mathbf{a}_{i}\in L_{h,k}}\Lambda_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{1-q}d\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2dNh_{\max}q_{\max}}{\delta}}\sqrt{s}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\lVert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rVert_{1}}{N}\frac{2d}{\sqrt{s}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2dNh_{\max}q_{\max}}{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\lVert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rVert_{1}}{Nh_{\max}q_{\max}}

as we have set

\frac{2d}{\sqrt{s}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2dNh_{\max}q_{\max}}{\delta}}\leqslant\frac{2d}{\sqrt{b}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2dNh_{\max}q_{\max}}{\delta}}\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{h_{\max}q_{\max}}.

Summing over the buckets
k\in[N]
and union bounding and summing over
h\in[h_{\max}]
yields the desired result.
∎

Next, we handle the remaining levels. We pay the price of having smaller hash buckets in the distortion at this point.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
q\in[q_{\max}]
. Then with probability at least
1-2\delta/q_{\max}
,

\sum_{h:p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert<bN}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\delta\alpha}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}

Proof.

Note that if
p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert\leqslant\delta/h_{\max}q_{\max}
, then by a union bound over the at most
h_{\max}
levels of
h
, none of these levels
h
sample any elements from weight class
q
with probability at least
\delta/q_{\max}
. Then for each weight class
q
, only the subsampling levels
h
for

\frac{\delta}{h_{\max}q_{\max}}\leqslant p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert\leqslant bN

can contribute to the mass of the sketch
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}
. Note that this is only

\log_{B}\left\lparen\frac{bNh_{\max}q_{\max}}{\delta}\right\rparen=\log_{B}\left[\poly(d,\log n,\delta^{-1})\right]=O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\alpha}\right\rparen

levels of subsampling, where each level contributes at most

\E\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\E\lVert\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}=\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}

in expectation. We thus conclude by summing over
h
with
p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert<bN
and then applying a Markov bound.
∎

Putting the above pieces together yield the following:

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

As previously discussed in this section, the “no contraction” direction of
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\Omega(1)\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}
is handled in Lemma
B.1
, so we focus on proving the “no dilation” direction of
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\geqslant O(1/\delta\alpha)\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}
.

We union bound and sum over the results from Lemmas
4.5
and
4.6
for
q\in[q_{\max}]
to see that with probability at least
1-3\delta
,

\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\delta\alpha}\right\rparen\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert.

We also note that
\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(0}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}
by the triangle inequality. Finally, we have that in expectation, the weight classes
q>q_{\max}
contribute at most

\displaystyle\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\E\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\lVert\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\frac{2\delta}{ndh_{\max}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{a})\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\delta\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}.

Then by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-\delta
, these levels contribute at most
2\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}
. Summing these three results, we find that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(0}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}+\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}+\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{A}^{(q)}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}+O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\delta\alpha}\right\rparen\sum_{q\in[q_{\max}]}\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\delta\alpha}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}

as desired.
∎

4.2
Lower bound

We show that for
d\times d
matrices, the above trade-off between the sketching dimension and distortion is nearly optimal, up to log factors. Note that for constant
\delta
, the above result gives a
d^{\alpha}\poly\log d
sized sketch with distortion
1/\alpha
. We show that with a sketch of size
r
, a distortion of
\Omega((\log d)/(\log r))
is necessary.

By Yao’s minimax principle, we assume that the
r\times d
sketch matrix
\mathbf{S}
is fixed, and show that the distortion is
\Omega((\log d)/(\log r))
with constant probability over a distribution over input matrices
\mathbf{A}
.

The following simple lemma is central to our analysis:

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be an
r\times n
matrix, and let
\mathbf{A}
be drawn as an
n\times d
matrix with all of its columns drawn as i.i.d. Cauchy variables. Then,

\ProbOp\left\{\Omega(d\log d)\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(d\log(rd))\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\right\}\geqslant\frac{99}{100}.

Proof.

The proof relies on standard tricks and is deferred to Appendix
C
.
∎

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a fixed
r\times d
matrix. Then there is a distribution
\mu
over
d\times d
matrices such that if

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\mu}\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\right\rparen\geqslant\frac{2}{3}

then
\kappa=\Omega((\log d)/(\log r))
.

Proof.

We draw our matrix
\mathbf{A}
from
\mu
as follows. Let
\mu_{1}
be the distribution that draws
\mathbf{A}
as a
d\times d
i.i.d. matrix with Cauchy entries, and let
\mu_{2}
be the distribution that draws
\mathbf{A}
with its first
r
columns as a
d\times r
i.i.d. matrix with Cauchy entries scaled by
d/r
, and the rest of the
d-r
columns all
0
s. Then,
\mu
draws from
\mu_{1}
with probability
1/2
and
\mu_{2}
with probability
1/2
.

Note that by Lemmas 2.10 and 2.12 of
[
WW19
]
,

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\mu_{1}}\left\lparen\Omega(d^{2}\log d)\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(d^{2}\log d)\right\rparen

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{99}{100}

\displaystyle\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\mu_{2}}\left\lparen\frac{d}{r}\Omega(rd\log(rd))\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{d}{r}O(rd\log(rd))\right\rparen

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{99}{100}

By Lemma
4.7
, if
\mathbf{A}\sim\mu_{1}
, then
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}=\Omega(d\log d)\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}
with probability at least
99/100
. Now suppose for contradiction that
\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}=\omega(\kappa d)
. Then with probability at least
1-(1/3+1/2+1/100+1/100)>0
, we have that

\omega(\kappa d^{2}\log d)=\Omega(d\log d)\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}=O(\kappa d^{2}\log d)

which is a contradiction. Thus,
\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}=O(\kappa d)
.

Now consider
\mathbf{A}\sim\mu_{2}
. By Lemma
4.7
,
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(r\log r)\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}
with probability at least
99/100
. Then, with probability at least
1-(1/3+1/2+1/100+1/100)>0
,

\Omega(d^{2}\log d)\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(d\log r)\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}=O(\kappa d^{2}\log r)

so

\kappa=\Omega\left\lparen\frac{\log d}{\log r}\right\rparen

as desired.
∎

5
Independence Testing in the
\ell_{1}
norm

In this section, we present our result for estimating
\lVert P-Q\rVert_{1}
, where
P
is the joint distribution and
Q
the product distribution defined by the marginals, which are determined by the stream items as introduced in Section
1
. We first prepare a heavy hitter data structure in Section
5.1
and present our
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm to the
\ell_{1}
norm of order-
2
tensors in Section
5.2
. To move to higher dimensions, we need a rough estimator for the product distribution in Section
5.3
. Finally, we apply the result for order-
2
tensors iteratively in Section
5.4
to obtain a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
\lVert P-Q\rVert_{1}
.

5.1
Heavy Hitters

This subsection is devoted to a data structure, called the
HeavyHitter
structure, which is analogous to the classical
CountSketch
data structure for a general functional
f
on a general linear space.

Suppose that
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is function satisfying the following properties:

1.

f(0)=0
;

2.

f(x)=f(-x)
;

3.

f(x)
is increasing on
[0,\infty)
;

4.

There exists a constant
C_{f}
such that it holds for any integer
s\geqslant 1
and any
x_{1},\dots,x_{s},y_{1},\dots,y_{s}\in\mathbb{R}
that
\sum_{i=1}^{s}f(x_{i}+y_{i})\leqslant C_{f}(\sum_{i=1}^{s}(f(x_{i})+f(y_{i})))
.

5.

There exists a function
h:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that

(a)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}h(\varepsilon)=0
;

(b)

it holds for any integer
s\geqslant 1
and any
x_{1},\dots,x_{s},y_{1},\dots,y_{s}\in\mathbb{R}
that
|\sum_{i=1}^{s}f(x_{i}+y_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{s}f(x_{i})|\leqslant h(\varepsilon)\sum_{i}f(x_{i})
whenever
\sum_{i}f(y_{i})\leqslant\varepsilon\sum_{i}f(x_{i})
.

We abuse notation and define for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
that
f(x)=\sum_{i}f(x_{i})
.

We define a different Rademacher dimension as follows. The Rademacher dimension
B=B(f;\eta)
is the smallest integer such that the following holds for any integer
s\geqslant 1
. Let
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{s}
be i.i.d. Rademacher variables and
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{s}
be i.i.d. Bernoulli variables such that
\E\xi_{i}=1/B
. It holds for any
x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
that

\ProbOp\left\{f\left(\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\xi_{i}x_{i}\right)\leqslant\eta\sum_{i}f(x_{i})\right\}\geqslant 0.9.

5.1
.

Let
\gamma,\zeta\in(0,1/3)
, there exists
r=r(\gamma,\zeta)
and a randomized linear map
T:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
, and a subrecovery algorithm
\mathcal{B}
such that for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, with probability at least
1-\zeta
, it holds that
(1-\gamma)f(x)\leqslant\mathcal{B}(Tx)\leqslant(1+\gamma)f(x)
.

Then, for
\theta,\delta\in(0,1/3)
, there exists a randomized linear function
M:(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{S}
, where
S=O(B\log(d/\delta)\cdot r(\gamma,\zeta^{\prime}))
for
B=B(f;h^{-1}(\theta)\theta)
and
\zeta^{\prime}=O(\zeta/(B\log(d/\delta))
, and a recovery algorithm
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the following. For any
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{d}
with probability
\geqslant 1-\delta-\zeta
,
\mathcal{A}
reads
Mx
and outputs an estimate
\tilde{f}_{i}
for each
i\in[d]
such that

1.

|\tilde{f}_{i}-f(x_{i})|\leqslant(\gamma+\theta+\gamma\theta)f(x_{i})
whenever
f(x_{i})\geqslant\theta f(x)
;

2.

|\tilde{f}_{i}|\leqslant C_{f}\theta(1+\gamma)(1+h(\theta))f(x)
whenever
f(x_{i})<\theta f(x)
.

Proof.

The linear sketch
M
is essentially a
CountSketch
data structure, which hashes
\{Tx_{i}\}_{i}
into
B=B(f;\min\{\varepsilon^{2},h(\theta)\}\eta)
buckets under a hash function
h
. The
b
-th bucket contains

T_{b}=\sum_{i:h(i)=b}\sigma_{i}Tx_{i}.

For
i^{\ast}
such that
f(x_{i^{\ast}})\geqslant\theta f(x)
, the algorithm will just return
\tilde{f}_{i^{\ast}}=\mathcal{B}(T_{h(i^{\ast})})
. Next we analyse the estimation error. Let
b=h(i^{\ast})
. Note that
\sum_{i:h(i)=b}\sigma_{i}Tx_{i}
is identically distributed as
Tx_{i^{\ast}}+T\nu
, where
\nu=\sum_{i\neq i^{\ast}:h(i)=b}\sigma_{i}x_{i}
. Since
B=B(f;h^{-1}(\theta)\theta)
, it holds with probability at least
0.9
that

f(\nu)\leqslant h^{-1}(\theta)\theta f(x)\leqslant h^{-1}(\theta)f(x_{i^{\ast}}),

which implies that

\left(1-\theta\right)f(x_{i^{\ast}})\leqslant f\left(x_{i^{\ast}}+\nu\right)\leqslant\left(1+\theta\right)f(x_{i^{\ast}}).

and, with probability at least
0.9-\zeta
that

(1-\gamma)(1-\theta)\mathcal{B}(Tx_{i^{\ast}})\leqslant\mathcal{B}(T_{b})\leqslant(1+\gamma)(1+\theta)\mathcal{B}(Tx_{i^{\ast}}).

On the other hand, when
f(x_{i^{\ast}})\leqslant\theta f(x)
,

f(x_{i^{\ast}}+\nu)\leqslant C_{f}(f(x_{i^{\ast}})+f(\nu))\leqslant C_{f}(\theta+h(\theta)\theta)f(x)

and, with probability at least
1-\zeta
,

\mathcal{B}(T_{b})\leqslant C_{f}\theta(1+\gamma)(1+h(\theta))f(x).

Repeat
\Theta(\log(d/\delta))
times to drive the failure probability down to
\delta/d
to take a union bound over all
i^{\ast}
.
∎

The data structure described in Lemma
5.1
is our
HeavyHitter
structure, parameterized with
(\theta,\delta)
.

1

2

Require:

\varepsilon,\delta,K,N,t,\zeta

3

L\leftarrow\log(KN/\varepsilon)

4

\hat{L}\leftarrow\log N

5

\theta\leftarrow\min\{\Theta(\varepsilon^{3}/(C_{f}L^{3})),h^{-1}(\alpha\varepsilon/3),\alpha\varepsilon/4\}

6

B\leftarrow B(f;h^{-1}(\theta)\theta\})

7

Q\leftarrow O(B(\hat{L}+1)\log(N\hat{L}))

8

Instantiate a subsampling function
H
, which hashes
[N]
into
\hat{L}
levels such that the sampling probability for the
\ell
-th level is
2^{-\ell}
and is pairwise independent

9

for

each
\ell=0,1,\dots,\hat{L}

do

10

Instantiate a
HeavyHitter
structure
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
with parameters
(\theta,0.05/(\hat{L}+1))
, in which each bucket stores a vector of length
t=t(\alpha\varepsilon/2,\zeta/Q)

11

end for

12

Algorithm 0

(Data Structure for constant failure probability algorithm (
SubsamplingHeavyHitter
))
.

1

2

Input:

an update of the form
x_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}+\Delta x_{i}

3

for

each
\ell=0,\dots,\hat{L}

do

4

if

H
hashes
i
into level
\ell

then

\triangleright

Assume
H
hashes every
i
to level
0

5

b_{\ell}\leftarrow
index of the bucket containing
i
in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}

6

Add
T(\Delta x_{i})
to the
b_{\ell}
-th bucket

7

end if

8

end for

9

Algorithm 0

(Algorithm for an update to
x_{i}
for our constant failure probability algorithm)
.

1

2

3

Require:

(i) A subsampling scheme
H
such that the
i
-th level has subsampling probability
p_{i}=2^{-i}
; (ii)
\hat{L}+1

HeavyHitter
structures
\mathcal{D}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{\hat{L}}
with the same parameters
(\theta,\delta)
, where
\hat{L}=\log N
,
\theta=\min\{\Theta(\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{C_{f}L^{3}}),\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{4},h^{-1}(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{3})\}
and
\delta=\frac{1}{20(\hat{L}+1)}
; (iii) an approximation
\widehat{M}
such that
M\leqslant\widehat{M}
; (iv) an integer
K\geqslant 2
which is a power of
2
.

4

L\leftarrow\log(2N/\varepsilon)

5

\hat{L}\leftarrow\log N

6

for

j=0,\dots,\hat{L}

do

7

\Lambda_{j}\leftarrow\text{top }\Theta(L^{3}/\varepsilon^{3})\text{ heavy hitters from }\mathcal{D}_{j}

8

end for

9

j_{0}\leftarrow\log(4K\varepsilon^{-3}L^{3})

10

\zeta\leftarrow
uniform variable in
[1/2,1]

11

for

j=0,\dots,j_{0}

do

12

Let
\lambda_{1}^{(j)},\dots,\lambda_{s}^{(j)}
be the elements in
\Lambda_{0}
contained in
[(1+\varepsilon)\zeta\frac{\widehat{M}}{2^{j}},(2-\varepsilon)\zeta\frac{\widehat{M}}{2^{j}}]

13

\widetilde{M}_{j}\leftarrow f(\lambda_{1}^{(j)})+\cdots+f(\lambda_{s}^{(j)})

14

end for

15

for

j=j_{0}+1,\dots,L

do

16

Find the biggest
\ell
such that
\Lambda_{\ell}
contains
s
elements
\lambda^{(j)}_{1},\dots,\lambda^{(j)}_{s}
in
[(1+\varepsilon)\zeta\frac{\widehat{M}}{2^{j}},(2-\varepsilon)\zeta\frac{\widehat{M}}{2^{j}}]
for
(1-\sqrt{20}\varepsilon)\frac{L^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leqslant s\leqslant 2(1+\sqrt{20}\varepsilon)\frac{L^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}

17

if

such
\ell
exists

then

18

\widetilde{M}_{j}\leftarrow(f(\lambda^{(j)}_{1})+\cdots+f(\lambda^{(j)}_{s}))2^{\ell}

19

else

20

\widetilde{M}_{j}\leftarrow 0

21

end if

22

end for

23

return

\widetilde{M}\leftarrow\sum_{j}\widetilde{M}_{j}

Algorithm 0

(
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximator to
f(x)
with constant failure probability)
.

5.2
(1+\varepsilon)
-Approximator

Suppose that for any
\gamma,\zeta\in(0,1)
that are small enough, there exist
t=t(\gamma,\zeta)
, a randomized linear map
T:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{t}
and a subrecovery algorithm
\mathcal{B}
such that for each
x\in X
, with probability at least
1-\zeta
, it holds that
(1-\gamma)f(x)\leqslant\mathcal{B}(Tx)\leqslant(1+\gamma)f(x)
.

Let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{N}
. In this subsection, we consider the problem of approximating
M=\sum_{i}f(x_{i})
up to a
(1+\varepsilon)
-factor. We also assume that we have an approximation
\widehat{M}
to
M
such that
M\leqslant\widehat{M}\leqslant KM
.

Our algorithm is inspired from arguments in
[
ABIW09
]
. We prepare the following data structure (Algorithm
5.1
) with the entry update algorithm (Algorithm
5.2
). The recovery algorithm is presented in Algorithm
5.3
.

5.2
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be small enough and
K\geqslant 2
be a power of
2
. Let
\theta,B,Q
be as defined in Algorithm
5.1
. There exists an absolute constant
\alpha<1
and a randomized linear sketch
\Pi:(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{S}
, where
S=O(Q\cdot t(\alpha\varepsilon/2,0.05/Q))
and a recovery algorithm
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the following.

For any
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in X^{N}
and an approximation
\widehat{M}\geqslant M=\sum_{i}f(x_{i})
, with probability at least
0.6
,
\mathcal{A}
reads
\Pi x
and outputs
\widetilde{M}(x)
such that

1.

(1-\varepsilon)M\leqslant\widetilde{M}(x)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)M
if
\widehat{M}\in[(K/2)M,KM]
;

2.

\widetilde{M}(x)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)M
otherwise.

Proof.

There are
\Theta(\log(1/\delta))
repetitions. In each repetition, there are
(\hat{L}+1)

HeavyHitter
structures of
O(B\log(N\hat{L}))
buckets. There are
O(B(\hat{L}+1)\log(N\hat{L}))
buckets in each repetition. Each bucket stores a sketch of length
t(\alpha\varepsilon/2,0.05/Q)
. The total space complexity follows.

Since for each bucket the failure probability is
0.05/Q
, we can take a union bound over all buckets and assume that
\mathcal{B}
gives accurate answers on all buckets in a repetition with probability at least
0.95
. Then the claimed result follows from Theorem
5.6
for
\hat{M}\in[(K/2)M,KM]
and from Lemma
5.11
for
\widehat{M}>KM
and Lemma
5.12
for
\widehat{M}<(K/2)M
.
∎

Next we extend the algorithm to handle the case where
\widehat{M}<(K/2)M
.

5.3
.

Let
\varepsilon,\theta,B,Q,S
be as in Theorem
5.2
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. There exists an absolute constant
\alpha<1
and a randomized linear sketch
\Pi:(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{S^{\prime}}
, where
S^{\prime}=O(S\log K\cdot\log(\delta^{-1}\log K))
and a recovery algorithm
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the following.

For any
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in X^{N}
and an approximation
\widehat{M}
such that
M\leqslant\widehat{M}\leqslant KM
, where
M=\sum_{i}f(x_{i})
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
\mathcal{A}
reads
\Pi x
and outputs
\widetilde{M}(x)
such that
(1-\varepsilon)M\leqslant\widetilde{M}(x)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)M
.

Proof.

First, in view of Theorem
5.2
, repeating the Algorithm
5.3

\Theta(\log(1/\zeta))
times and taking the median reduces the failure probability of a single run to
\zeta
. Hence, with sketch length
O(S\log(1/\zeta))
, we have an algorithm outputting
\widetilde{M}
such that
(1-\varepsilon)M\leqslant\widetilde{M}(x)\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)M
, provided that
(K/2)M\leqslant\widehat{M}\leqslant KM
.

For a general
\widehat{M}
, we run
\log K
instances of the aforesaid algorithm in parallel, where the parameter
K
in Algorithm
5.3
takes values
2,4,8,\dots,K
, respectively. Note that
\widehat{M}\in[(K/2)M,KM]
in one of these instances and, with probability at least
1-\zeta
, the output
\widehat{M}
of this instance satisfies that
\widehat{M}\in[(1-\varepsilon)M,(1+\varepsilon)M]
. For each other instance, with probability at
1-\zeta
, the outputted
\widehat{M}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)M
. Setting
\zeta=\delta/(\log K)
and taking the maximum output
\widehat{M}
among the
\log K
instances with a union bound over
\log K
instances, we obtain an estimate in
[(1-\varepsilon)M,(1+\varepsilon)M]
with probability at least
1-\delta
, as desired.
∎

5.3
Rough Approximator for
\ell_{1}
-Norm

Consider the problem of estimating
\|x\|_{1}
up to a constant factor for
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d^{q}}
in the turnstile streaming model, where each update changes a coordinate by a
+1
or a
-1
. Let
N=d^{q}
.
The following result is due to Braverman and Ostrovsky
[
BO
]
.

5.4
Rough approximation; Corollary 6.6 and Lemma 6.7 in
[
BO
]
.

There exists a randomized linear sketch
\Pi:\mathbb{Z}^{N}\to\mathbb{Z}^{S}
for
S=\tilde{O}(q\log(md))
and a recovery algorithm
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the following. For any
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
given in the aforementioned turnstile streaming model of length
m
, with probability at least
0.95
,
\mathcal{A}
reads
\Pi x
and outputs
\widehat{M}
such that
\|x\|_{1}\leqslant\widehat{M}\leqslant 4^{q^{2}}(\log d)^{q}\|x\|_{1}
.

5.4
Estimation of Total Variation Distance

Now we wish to estimate
\|P-Q\|_{1}
. Recall that
P
is a general joint distribution and
Q
the product distribution induced by the marginals of
P
.

We apply the data structure iteratively in Section
5.2
. For
\ell_{1}
-norm,
f(x)=h(x)=x
,
C_{f}=1
,
B(f;\varepsilon)=\Theta(1/\varepsilon^{2})
. Therefore, in Theorem
5.3
, one can take
B_{i}=(L/\varepsilon)^{c}
for some absolute constant
c\geqslant 4
. The basic setup is presented in Algorithm
5.4
. For each

, we apply Theorem
5.3
and obtain a linear sketch
\Pi^{(i)}
and a recovery algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. The sub-recovery algorithm for
\mathcal{D}^{(i)}_{\ell}
is
\mathcal{A}_{i-1}
. The entry update calls
EntryUpdate
(
i_{1},\dots,i_{q},\Delta,q
) on the final sketch (see Algorithm
5.5
) if there is an entry update of
\Delta
at position
(i_{1},\dots,i_{q})
. For notational convenience, we assume it is always true that a subsampling hash function hashes all coordinates into level
0
. The overall decoding algorithm calls
Decode
(
q
), see Algorithm
5.6
.

Algorithm 0

(Tensorization of
Q
: construct the sketch for
P_{1}^{f}\otimes\cdots\otimes P_{n}^{f}
)
.

5.5
.

Suppose that the stream length
m=\poly(d^{q})
. There is a randomized sketching algorithm which outputs a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-approximation to
\|P-Q\|_{1}
with probability at least
0.9
, using
\exp(O(q^{2}+q\log(q/\varepsilon)+q\log\log d))
bits of space. The update time is
\exp(O(q^{2}+q\log(q/\varepsilon)+q\log\log d))
.

Proof.

Let
P^{f}
be the frequency vector of the empirical distribution of the input stream and
P_{i}^{f}
be the corresponding frequency vector for the marginal on
X_{i}
. We have
P=P^{f}/m
and
P_{i}=P_{i}^{f}/m
.

Let
\Pi^{(q)}
be the final linear sketch described above. In parallel we run the rough approximator (Theorem
5.4
), which applies in our setting because the stream items are samples from the distribution and we are counting the empirical frequency. We maintain
\Pi(P^{f})
as described in Algorithm
5.2
. For the marginals
P_{i}^{f}
, we maintain sketches
S^{(i)}_{0}P_{i}^{f},\dots,S^{(i)}_{L^{\prime}}P_{i}^{f}
as in Algorithm
5.7
and Algorithm
5.8
. At the end of the stream, we construct
\Pi^{(q)}Q^{f}
for
Q^{f}=P_{1}^{f}\otimes\cdots\otimes P_{n}^{f}
as in Algorithm
5.9
. Then we compute
m^{q-1}\Pi^{(q)}P^{f}-\Pi^{(q)}Q^{f}=m^{q}\Pi(P-Q)
, from which we can recover an approximation to
\|P-Q\|_{1}
by invoking
\mathcal{A}_{q}
.

Next we analyze the space complexity. Let
N_{i}=d
. Since we are sketching
m^{q}(P-Q)
, whose
\ell_{1}
norm is an integer and is at most
2m^{q}
, we see that
K\leqslant 2m^{q}=d^{\Theta(q^{2})}
by our assumption that
m=\poly(d^{q})
. Set
\varepsilon_{q}=\varepsilon
and
\delta_{q}=O(1)
, then

\varepsilon_{i-1}=\alpha\varepsilon_{i},\quad\delta_{i-1}=\poly\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{i}}{\log(KN_{i}/\varepsilon_{i})}\right)

for all
i
. This implies that

\varepsilon_{q-i}=\alpha^{i-1}\varepsilon,\quad\delta_{q-i}=\frac{\varepsilon^{\Theta(i)}}{q^{\Theta(i)}\log^{\Theta(i)}(\frac{qd}{\varepsilon})}

Therefore the target dimension of
\Pi^{(i)}
is

\displaystyle t_{i+1}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\left(\frac{\log(KN_{i}/\varepsilon_{i+1})}{\varepsilon_{i+1}}\right)^{c}\cdot t_{i}\cdot\log K\log\left(\frac{K}{\delta_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{\prime}\left(\frac{q^{2}}{\alpha^{q-i}\varepsilon}\right)^{\Theta(1)}\cdot t_{i}\cdot(q-i)\polylog\left(\frac{qd}{\varepsilon}\right)

with
t_{1}=1
. This implies that

t_{n}\leqslant(C^{\prime})^{q}\frac{q^{\Theta(q)}}{\alpha^{\Theta(q^{2})}\varepsilon^{\Theta(q)}}\cdot q!\cdot\log^{O(q)}\left(\frac{qd}{\varepsilon}\right)=\exp\left(O\left(q^{2}+q\log\frac{q}{\varepsilon}+q\log\log d\right)\right).

This space dominates the space needed by the rough estimator. Each coordinate requires
O(\log(m^{q}))=O(q^{2}\log d)
bits and the overall space complexity (in bits) follows.

The update time is clearly dominated by the update time for
P
, which is dominated by the sketch length.
∎

5.5
Correctness of Algorithm
5.3

We adopt the notation from Section
5.2
. Recall that our goal is to estimate
M=\sum_{i}f(x_{i})
up to a
(1+\varepsilon)
-factor and we also assume that we have an approximation
\widehat{M}
to
M
which satisfies that
(K/2)M\leqslant\widehat{M}\leqslant KM
.

Let
\zeta
be a uniform random variable on
[1/2,1]
. For a magnitude level
j
, define

T_{j}=\zeta\frac{\widehat{M}}{2^{j}},

and

S_{j}=\left\{i\in U:f(x_{i})\in\left(T_{j},2T_{j}\right]\right\},\quad s_{j}=|S_{j}|.

Observe that if we scale
K
by a factor of
2^{t}
, the magnitude levels are shifted by
t
levels (new top levels are empty). It is easy to see that the behaviour of Algorithm
5.3
is invariant under the concurrent scaling of
K
and shifting of the magnitude levels (since the bucket contents in the
HeavyHitter
structures remain the same), we may, with loss of generality, assume that
K=2
and
M\leqslant\widehat{M}\leqslant 2M
.

Observe that
\sum_{j\geqslant 1}\sum_{i\in S_{j}}f(x_{i})=M
. Note that each element in level
j>\log(2N/\varepsilon)
is at most
\widehat{M}/2^{j}<(\varepsilon/(2N))\widehat{M}<(\varepsilon/N)M
, so it contribute at most
\varepsilon M
and thus can be omitted. That is, we only need to consider the levels up to
L=\log(2N/\varepsilon)
.

We call a level
j
important if

\frac{s_{j}}{2^{j}}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2L}

and we let
\mathcal{J}
denote the set of important levels
j
. The non-important levels contributes at most

\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i\in S_{j}}f(x_{i})\leqslant\sum_{j\not\in\mathcal{J}}\frac{2\varepsilon}{2L}M\leqslant\varepsilon\widehat{M}\leqslant 2\varepsilon M.

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.

5.6
.

Algorithm
5.3
returns an estimate
\widetilde{M}
, which with probability at least
0.7
(over
\zeta
and subsampling) satisfies that

(1-O(\varepsilon))M\leqslant\widetilde{M}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))M.

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of the theorem. We assume that all
Count-Min
structures return correct values, at the loss of
0.05
probability. The main argument is decomposed into the following lemmas.

5.7
.

With probability at least
0.95
(over subsampling), the following holds for all
j>j_{0}
and
j\in\mathcal{J}
. There exists an
\ell
such that the substream induced by
H_{\ell}
contains at least
(1-O(\varepsilon))L^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
and at most
2(1+O(\varepsilon))L^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
elements of
S_{j}
. Furthermore, it holds
\frac{3}{4}L^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}\leqslant s_{j}2^{-\ell}\leqslant\frac{9}{4}L^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
for any such
\ell
.

Proof.

Since
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
j>j_{0}
,

s_{j}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2L}\cdot 2^{j_{0}}=\frac{4}{\varepsilon^{2}}L^{2}.

Let
N_{j,\ell}
denote the number of survivors in the
\ell
-th subsampling level. For
\ell=1
, we have

\E N_{j,\ell}=s_{j}2^{-\ell}\geqslant\frac{4L^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}2^{-\ell}=\frac{2L^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Note that
s_{j}\leqslant 2^{j}
, and thus for
\ell=j-\log(\varepsilon^{-2}L^{2})>0
, we have

\E N_{j,\ell}=s_{j}2^{-\ell}\leqslant 2^{j-\ell}=\varepsilon^{-2}L^{2}

survivors after sampling. Hence, there exists
\ell
such that
\varepsilon^{-2}L^{2}\leqslant\E N_{j,\ell}\leqslant 2\varepsilon^{-2}L^{2}
. For any such
\ell
, since
H_{\ell}
is pairwise independent, we have
\Var(N_{j,\ell})\leqslant\E N_{j,\ell}
and it follows from Chebyshev’s inequality that with probability at least
1-1/(20L^{2})
,

N_{j,\ell}=\E N_{j,\ell}\pm\sqrt{20L^{2}\cdot\E N_{j,\ell}},

that is,

(1-\sqrt{20}\varepsilon)\frac{L^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leqslant N_{j,\ell}\leqslant 2(1+\sqrt{20}\varepsilon)\frac{L^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

(2)

A similar argument shows that for each
\ell
, with probability at least
1-1/(20L^{2})
, we have
N_{j,\ell}\geqslant\frac{9}{4}(1-\sqrt{20}\varepsilon)L^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
if
\E N_{j,\ell}\geqslant\frac{9}{4}L^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
and
N_{j,\ell}\leqslant\frac{3}{4}(1+\sqrt{20}\varepsilon)L^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
if
\E N_{j,\ell}\leqslant\frac{3}{4}L^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
. Taking a union bound over all
L
, we have that with probability at least
1-1/(20L)
there exists a unique
\ell
such that (
2
) holds; furthermore,
s_{j}2^{-\ell}=\E N_{j,\ell}=\Theta(\varepsilon^{-2}L^{2})
for this
\ell
.

The claimed result follows from a union bound over
j
.
∎

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
be a small constant. Define

\displaystyle S_{j}^{\ast}=\left\{i\in S_{j}:f(x_{i})\in[(1+(1-\alpha)\varepsilon)T_{j},(2-(1-\alpha)\varepsilon)T_{j}]\right\}

\displaystyle S_{j}^{\ast\ast}=\left\{i\in S_{j}:f(x_{i})\in[(1+(1+\alpha)\varepsilon)T_{j},(2-(1+\alpha)\varepsilon)T_{j}]\right\}

and

M_{j}^{\ast}=\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast}}f(x_{i}),\quad M_{j}^{\ast\ast}=\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast\ast}}f(x_{i}).

Suppose that the event in Lemma
5.7
occurs.

5.8
.

With probability at least
0.9
(over the subsamplings), it holds for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
j>j_{0}
that
(1-O(\eta))M_{j}\leqslant\E\widetilde{M}_{j}\leqslant(1+O(\eta))
, where the expectation is taken over the subsampling.

Proof.

Let
I_{\ell}\subseteq[N]
be the set of indices in subsampling level
\ell
, where
\ell
is found in Step
5.3
of Algorithm
5.3
. Then

\E f(x_{I_{\ell}})=\frac{f(x)}{2^{\ell}}.

By Lemma
5.7
and our choice of
\beta
, we have

\frac{s_{j}}{2^{\ell}}\leqslant\frac{9}{4}\cdot\frac{L^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

(3)

Together with the assumption that
j\in\mathcal{J}
,

2^{j}\leqslant\frac{L}{\varepsilon}s_{j}\leqslant\frac{9}{4}\cdot\frac{L^{3}}{\varepsilon^{3}}2^{\ell},

which implies that (by adjusting constants)

\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{L^{3}}\E f(x_{I_{\ell}})\leqslant\frac{9}{4}\frac{f(x)}{2^{j}}=\frac{9}{4}\frac{1}{\zeta}T_{j}\leqslant\frac{9}{2}T_{j}.

Except with probability
0.05/L
, we have

\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{180L^{3}}f(x_{I_{\ell}})\leqslant T_{j}.

Now, let
\theta=\min\{\varepsilon^{3}/(180C_{f}L^{3}),\alpha\varepsilon/4,h^{-1}(\alpha\varepsilon/3)\}
in Lemma
5.1
, we have the guarantees that (1) if
f(x_{i})\geqslant\theta T_{j}
then it is estimated up to an additive error of at most

(\gamma+\theta+\gamma\theta)f(x_{i})\leqslant(\gamma+2\theta)f(x_{i})\leqslant\left(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{2}+2\cdot\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{4}\right)f(x_{i})=\alpha\varepsilon f(x_{i}),

and (2) if
f(x_{i})\leqslant\theta T_{j}
we obtain an estimate at most

\displaystyle C_{f}\theta(1+\gamma)(1+h(\theta))f(x_{I_{\ell}})\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{40L^{3}}(1+\gamma)(1+h(\theta))f(x_{I_{\ell}})

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\gamma)(1+h(\theta))T_{j}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(1+\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{2}\right)\left(1+\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{3}\right)T_{j}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\alpha\varepsilon)T_{j}.

Hence, all survivors in level
S_{j}^{\ast\ast}
will be recovered and all survivors in the higher levels will not be mistakenly recovered in level
j
; survivors from lower levels will not collude to form a heavy hitter.

Let
R^{(j)}=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\}
, we have
\lambda^{(j)}_{r}=(1+O(\varepsilon))f(x_{i_{r}})
for all
r\in[s]
. Then

\widetilde{M}_{j}=(\lambda^{(j)}_{1}+\cdots+\lambda^{(j)}_{s})2^{\ell}=(1\pm(O(\varepsilon))\widetilde{M}_{j}^{\prime},

where

\widetilde{M}_{j}^{\prime}=2^{\ell}\sum_{r=1}^{s}f(x_{i_{r}})

will be our focus. Combining Lemma
5.7
with the recovery guarantee of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
, we see that all elements in
S_{j}
that survives the subsampling at level
\ell
will be recovered. Hence,
\ProbOp\{i\in R^{(j)}\}\leqslant 2^{-\ell}
for
i\in S_{j}^{\ast}
(because it may not be recovered in our range) and
\ProbOp\{i\in R^{(j)}\}=2^{-\ell}
for
i\in S_{j}^{\ast\ast}
(because if it survives the subsampling it would be recovered). Hence

\E\widetilde{M}_{j}^{\prime}=2^{\ell}\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast}}f(x_{i})\ProbOp\{i\in R^{(j)}\}\leqslant 2^{\ell}\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast}}f(x_{i})2^{-\ell}=M_{j}^{\ast}

and

\E\widetilde{M}_{j}^{\prime}\geqslant 2^{\ell}\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast\ast}}f(x_{i})\ProbOp\{i\in R^{(j)}\}=2^{\ell}\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast\ast}}f(x_{i})2^{-\ell}=M_{j}^{\ast\ast}

∎

5.9
.

With probability at least
0.95
(over the subsamplings), it holds for all
j\leqslant j_{0}
that
(1-O(\varepsilon))M_{j}^{\ast\ast}\leqslant\widetilde{M}_{j}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))M_{j}^{\ast}
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to the preceding lemma. Note that there are at most
2^{j_{0}+1}=4L^{3}/\varepsilon^{3}
elements of interest in this case, and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is guaranteed to recover all of them, since

f(x_{i})\geqslant\xi 2^{-j_{0}}f(x)\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{4L^{3}}f(x)

and we choose
\theta=\min\{\varepsilon^{3}/(4C_{f}L^{3}),\alpha\varepsilon/4,h^{-1}(\alpha\varepsilon/3)\}
for
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, where
C
is an absolute constant. Each
f(x_{i})
is estimated up to an
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-factor.
∎

5.10
.

With probability at least
0.8
(over subsamplings) it holds that

(1-O(\varepsilon))\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}M_{j}^{\ast\ast}-O(\varepsilon M)\leqslant\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\widetilde{M}_{j}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))M.

Proof.

Note that
f(x_{i})
are within a factor of
2
from each other for
i\in S_{j}^{\ast}
, thus

\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast}}f(x_{i})^{2}\leqslant\frac{4}{|S_{j}^{\ast}|}\left(\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast}}f(x_{i})\right)^{2}=\frac{4}{|S_{j}^{\ast}|}(M_{j}^{\ast})^{2}.

When
j>j_{0}
and
j\in\mathcal{J}
, we showed that
|S_{j}^{\ast}|\geqslant\varepsilon^{-2}L^{2}
(Lemma
5.7
), thus

\sum_{i\in S_{j}^{\ast}}f(x_{i})^{2}=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{L^{2}}(M_{j}^{\ast})^{2}\right).

It follows from Chebyshev’s inequality that with probability at least
0.95
,

\left|\sum_{j>j_{0},j\in\mathcal{J}}\widetilde{M}_{j}-\E\sum_{j>j_{0},j\in\mathcal{J}}\widetilde{M}_{j}\right|=O\left(\varepsilon\sum_{j>j_{0},j\in\mathcal{J}}M_{j}^{\ast}\right)

Combining with Lemma
5.8
, we have with probability at least
0.85
,

(1-O(\varepsilon))\sum_{j>j_{0},j\in\mathcal{J}}M_{j}^{\ast\ast}-O(\varepsilon)\sum_{j>j_{0},j\in\mathcal{J}}M_{j}^{\ast}\leqslant\sum_{j>j_{0},j\in\mathcal{J}}\widetilde{M}_{j}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\sum_{j>j_{0},j\in\mathcal{J}}M_{j}^{\ast}

Further combining with Lemma
5.9
, we have with probability at least
0.8
,

(1-O(\varepsilon))\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}M_{j}^{\ast\ast}-O(\varepsilon)\sum_{j>j_{0},j\in\mathcal{J}}M_{j}^{\ast}\leqslant\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\widetilde{M}_{j}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}M_{j}^{\ast}

The result follows from the observation that
\sum_{j}M_{j}^{\ast}\leqslant M
.
∎

Note that the levels
j\not\in\mathcal{J}
contribute at most
O(\varepsilon M)
in expectation to the total norm. By Markov’s inequality, except with probability 0.05 (over subsampling), they contribute at most
O(\varepsilon M)
. Combining with the preceding lemma, we have concluded that with probability at least
0.75
,

(1-O(\varepsilon))\sum_{j\geqslant 1}M_{j}^{\ast\ast}-O(\varepsilon M)\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 1}\widetilde{M}_{j}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))M.

Over the randomness of
\zeta
, for each
i
, with probability at least
1-O(\varepsilon)
, we have
i\in S_{j^{\prime}}^{\ast\ast}
for some
j^{\prime}
. This implies that

\E\left(M-\sum_{j\geqslant 1}M_{j}^{\ast\ast}\right)=O(\varepsilon M).

By Markov’s inequality, we have with probability (over
\zeta
) at least
0.95
that

(1-O(\varepsilon))M\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 1}M_{j}^{\ast\ast}\leqslant M.

Finally, combining with the failure probability of the
HeavyHitter
structures, we conclude that with probability at least
0.7
,

(1-O(\varepsilon))M\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 1}\widetilde{M}_{j}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))M.

5.6
Analysis of Algorithm
5.3
with Bad
\widehat{M}

We have proved that Algorithm
5.3
, when provided a good overestimate
\widehat{M}
, gives a good estimate
\widetilde{M}
to
M
in the preceding Section
5.5
. In this section, we show that the algorithm does not overestimate when
\widehat{M}
is bad. We follow the notations in the preceding section and assume likewise that
K=2
.

5.11
.

Suppose that
\widehat{M}>2M
. Algorithm
5.3
returns an estimate
\widetilde{M}
, which with probability at least
0.7
(over
\zeta
and subsampling) satisfies that
\widetilde{M}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))M
.

Proof.

There exists
j^{\ast}<K
such that
\widehat{M}^{\ast}=\widehat{M}/2^{j^{\ast}}\in[M,2M]
. We compare the behavior of Algorithm
5.3
on estimate
\widehat{M}
and
\widehat{M}^{\ast}
, under the same randomness in the subsampling functions, heavy hitter structures and
\zeta
.
Denote the magnitude levels associated with
\widehat{M}^{\ast}
by
S^{\ast}_{1},S^{\ast}_{2},\dots
and the levels associated with
\widehat{M}
by
S_{1},S_{2}\dots
. It is clear that
S_{1}=\cdots=S_{j^{\ast}}=\emptyset
and
S_{j}=S^{\ast}_{j-j^{\ast}}
for
j>j^{\ast}
. Hence for
j\leqslant j_{0}
, we can still recover all items in
S^{\ast}_{1},\dots,S^{\ast}_{j_{0}^{\ast}}
for
j_{0}^{\ast}=j_{0}-j^{\ast}
, that is, all items in
S_{1},\dots,S_{j_{0}^{\ast}+j^{\ast}}
. Observe that
j^{\ast}<\log K
and so
j_{0}^{\ast}+j^{\ast}<j_{0}
, and so it is possible that we miss the levels
S_{j}
for
j=j_{0}^{\ast}+j^{\ast}+1,\dots,j_{0}
since the subsequent for-loop starts with
S_{j_{0}+1}
. All the recovered levels are within
(1\pm O(\varepsilon))
-factor of their true values, according to the proof of Theorem
5.6
, with probability at least
0.7
. Therefore, we shall never overestimate, that is,
\widetilde{M}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))M
.
∎

5.12
.

Suppose that
\widehat{M}<M
. Algorithm
5.3
returns an estimate
\widetilde{M}
, which with probability at least
0.7
(over
\zeta
and subsampling) satisfies that
\widetilde{M}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))M
.

Proof.

There exists
j^{\ast}
such that
2^{j^{\ast}}\widehat{M}=\widehat{M}^{\ast}\in[M,2M]
. Similar to the proof of Lemma
5.11
, we compare the behavior of Algorithm
5.3
on estimate
\widehat{M}
and
\widehat{M}^{\ast}
, under the same randomness in the subsampling functions, heavy hitter structures and
\zeta
. Let
\{S_{j}^{\ast}\}
and
\{S_{j}\}
be as defined in the proof of Lemma
5.11
. Now we have
S_{j}=S^{\ast}_{j+j^{\ast}}
and may miss the bands
S^{\ast}_{1},\dots,S^{\ast}_{j^{\ast}}
. The rest follows as in Lemma
5.11
.
∎

6
\ell_{1}
Subspace Embeddings for i.i.d. Random Design Matrices

In this section we present oblivious
\ell_{1}
subspace embeddings for i.i.d. random design matrices. This allows us to achieve a polynomial-sized sketch without paying the general case distortion lower bound of
\Omega(d/\log^{2}r)
of
[
WW19
]
.

In consideration of practical applications, we specifically focus on
heavy-tailed distributions
. In fact, as we will see, these are the most interesting from a theoretical perspective as well. Our model for our heavy-tailed distributions will be symmetric power law distributions of index
p
, which are distributions that satisfy

1-F(x)\sim cx^{-p}

for a constant
c
. In the literature, works such as
[
ZZ18
,
BE18
]
have considered linear regression in the
\ell_{1}
norm with heavy tailed i.i.d. design matrices.

p

Distortion lower bound

p\in(0,1)

O(1)
(Theorem
6.8
)

1

p=1

O\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\log(r/d^{2}\log d)}\right\rparen
(Theorem
6.9
)

\Omega\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\log r}\right\rparen
(Theorem
6.24
)

p\in(1,2)

O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/p}}{(r/d^{2})^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen
,
n^{1-1/p}>d^{1/p}\log d
(Theorem
6.18
)

O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen
,
n^{1-1/p}\leqslant d^{1/p}\log d
(Theorem
6.19
)

\Omega\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/p}}{r^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen
(Theorem
6.25
)

p\geqslant 2

1+\varepsilon
(Theorem
6.23
)

1

Table 1:
Results for i.i.d. symmetric power law design matrices

Throughout this section, let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law distribution with index
p\geqslant 0
, and let
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n\times d}
be a matrix drawn with i.i.d. entries drawn from
\mathcal{D}
, unless noted otherwise.

6.1
Setup for analysis

Let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a vector. We will frequently refer to the
k
th level set
\mathbf{v}_{(k)}
of
\mathbf{v}
, which takes on the values of
\mathbf{v}
whenever it has absolute value in
[2^{k},2^{k+1})
, and
0
otherwise.

Definition 6.1

(Level sets of a vector)
.

We define the
k
th level set
\mathbf{v}_{(k)}
of
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
coordinate-wise by

\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{(k)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}&\text{if $\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}\rvert\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

For
k=0
, we set

\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{(0)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}&\text{if $\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{v}\rvert\in[0,2)$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

We will repeatedly make use of the following simple lemmas about
CountSketch
and symmetric power law distributions.

Lemma 6.2

(No expansion)
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be drawn as an
r\times n

CountSketch
matrix with random signs
\sigma:[n]\to\{\pm 1\}
and hash functions
h:[n]\to[r]
. Then for all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}.

Proof.

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lvert\sum_{j:h(j)=i}^{n}\sigma_{i}v_{i}\right\rvert\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j:h(j)=i}^{n}\lvert\sigma_{i}v_{i}\rvert=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lvert v_{i}\rvert=\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}\qed

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law distribution with index
p>0
. Then, for
k
a large enough constant depending on
\mathcal{D}
,

\ProbOp_{X\sim\mathcal{D}}\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})\right\rparen=\Theta(2^{-kp})

and

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n}}\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{v}_{(k)}\rVert_{0}=\Theta(n2^{-kp})\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\exp\left\lparen\Theta(n2^{-kp})\right\rparen

Proof.

For a large enough
k
, we have that

\ProbOp_{X\sim\mathcal{D}}\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\in[2^{k},2^{k+1})\right\rparen=\overline{F}(2^{k})-\overline{F}(2^{k})=\Theta\left\lparen\frac{1}{2^{{k}p}}-\frac{1}{2^{{(k+1)}p}}\right\rparen=\Theta(2^{-kp}).

Then in expectation,

\lVert\mathbf{v}_{(k)}\rVert_{0}=\Theta(n2^{-kp})

so we conclude by Chernoff bounds.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law distribution with index
p>0
. Then,

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n\times d}}\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant O\left\lparen\left\lparen nd/\delta\right\rparen^{1/p}\right\rparen\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\delta

Proof.

Each entry is at most
O((nd/\delta)^{1/p})
with probability at least
\delta/nd
, so we conclude by a union bound over the
nd
entries.
∎

Definition 6.5

(Truncation)
.

For
T>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, define

\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}x&\text{$\lvert x\rvert\leqslant T$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

For a distribution
\mathcal{D}
, we define
\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(\mathcal{D})
to be the distribution that draws
\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(X)
for
X\sim\mathcal{D}
.

Lemma 6.6

(Moments of truncated power laws)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a power law distribution with index
p>0
. Let
T>0
be sufficiently large. Then

\displaystyle\E_{X\sim\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(\mathcal{D})}\lvert X\rvert

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Theta(T^{1-p})&\text{if $p\in(0,1)$}\\
\Theta(\log T)&\text{if $p=1$}\\
\Theta(1)&\text{if $p>1$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\E_{X\sim\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(\mathcal{D})}X^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Theta(T^{2-p})&\text{if $p\in(0,2)$}\\
\Theta(\log T)&\text{if $p=2$}\\
\Theta(1)&\text{if $p>2$}\end{cases}

Proof.

The proof is deferred to Appendix
D
.
∎

Definition 6.7
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
T>0
. Then, we write
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{H}+\mathbf{A}^{L}
where
\mathbf{A}^{H}
is the submatrix of
\mathbf{A}
formed by the rows containing an entry with absolute value at least
T
, and
\mathbf{A}^{L}
is the rest of the rows.

6.2
Algorithms for
p<1

We first present the results that for tails that are very heavy admit
O(1)
distortion embeddings in
\poly(d)
dimensions for a very simple reason: when
p<1
, then the largest entry in every vector is a good approximation of the entire
\ell_{1}
mass of the vector.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law distribution with index
p\in(0,1)
. Let
\mathbf{S}
be drawn as a
CountSketch
matrix with
r=O(d^{2}\log^{2}d)
rows. Then

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{S},\mathbf{A}}\left\lparen\Omega(1)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1},\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\rparen\geqslant\frac{99}{100}.

Proof.

The proof proceeds similarly to the case of
p=1
, and is deferred to Appendix
D
.
∎

Thus, we focus on the regime of
p\geqslant 1
.

6.3
Algorithms for
p=1

In this section, we prove the following:

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law distribution with index
p=1
. Let
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
be drawn as a
CountSketch
matrix. Then, for any
C(d\log d)^{2}\leqslant r\leqslant o(\sqrt{n})
for
C
a large enough constant, we have

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n\times d}}\left\{\frac{1}{\kappa}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1},\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}\geqslant\frac{99}{100}

for

\kappa=O\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\log(r/d^{2}\log d)}\right\rparen.

The idea is that with
r
rows of
CountSketch
, we can preserve the top
r
entries of
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
, which has mass approximately
\Omega(n\log r)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}
, while the rest of the entries have mass at most
O(n\log n)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}
. We formalize this idea in the following several lemmas.

Lemma 6.10

(Mass of small entries)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a power law distribution with index
p=1
and let
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n\times d}
. Let
\poly(d)\leqslant T\leqslant n
and let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{H}+\mathbf{A}^{L}
as in Definition
6.7
. Then,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(n\log T)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1},\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\rparen\geqslant 0.99

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{A}^{L}
is drawn i.i.d. from
\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(\mathcal{D})
so by Lemma
6.6
, it has entries with first two moments

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\coloneqq\E_{X\sim\mathcal{D}}\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\mid\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T\right\rparen=\Theta(\log T)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\E_{X\sim\mathcal{D}}\left\lparen X^{2}\mid\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T\right\rparen=\Theta(T)

Then for a single column
\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}
for
j\in[d]
, by Bernstein’s inequality,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\geqslant 2\mu n\right\rparen\leqslant\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\mu n)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}n+\mu nT/3}\right\rparen\leqslant\exp(-\Omega(\log T))=\frac{1}{\poly(T)}.

Then since
T\geqslant\poly(d)
, we may union bound over the
d
columns so that
\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}=O(\mu n)=O(n\log T)
for all columns
j\in[d]
with probability at least
1-1/\poly(d)
. Conditioned on this event, we have for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that

\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}=O(n\log T)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.\qed

Lemma 6.11

(Unique hashing of large entry rows)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a power law distribution with index
p>0
and cdf
F
, and let
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n\times d}
. Let
t
and
r
be parameters such that
r\geqslant C(d\log d)^{2}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
and
r=o(\sqrt{n})
, and define

\displaystyle\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq F^{-1}\left\lparen 1-\frac{C^{\prime}\log d}{n}\right\rparen=\Theta((n/\log d)^{1/p})

\displaystyle\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq F^{-1}\left\lparen 1-\frac{r/d^{2}\log d}{n}\right\rparen=\Theta((nd^{2}\log d/r)^{1/p})

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\exists j\in[d],\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert>\tau_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\exists j\in[d],\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert>\tau_{2}\right\}

for a sufficiently large constant
C^{\prime}
. Then if we hash each row of
\mathbf{A}
into
O(r)
hash buckets, with probability at least
0.95
:

–

Every row of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is hashed to a bucket with no other row from
\mathcal{R}_{2}
.

–

For every column
j\in[d]
and every integer
\log_{2}\tau_{2}\leqslant k\leqslant\log_{2}\tau_{1}
has
\Theta(n/2^{kp})
rows with a large entry with absolute value in
[2^{k},2^{k+1})
that are hashed to a bucket with no other row from
\mathcal{R}_{2}
.

–

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of rows which are hashed with no other row from
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, which we refer to as
uniquely hashed
rows. Then
\lvert\mathcal{R}\rvert=\Theta(r/d\log d)
and every one of these large entries is on a distinct row.

Proof.

For each
j\in[d]
, the number of expected entries in the
j
th column with absolute value at least
\tau_{1}
is
C^{\prime}\log d
, so by Chernoff bounds, with probability at least
1-\exp(\Theta(\log d))=1-1/\poly(d)
, there are at most
O(\log d)
such entries. By a union bound over the
d
columns, this is true for all
d
columns with probability at least
0.99
.

Similarly, for
\log_{2}\tau_{2}\leqslant k\leqslant\log_{2}\tau_{1}
, we have by Lemma
6.3
that

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})_{(k)}\rVert_{0}=\Theta(n/2^{kp})\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\exp\left\lparen\Theta(n/2^{kp})\right\rparen.

Then summing over
k
, we have that

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left\lparen\bigcap_{k=\log_{2}\tau_{2}}^{\log_{2}\tau_{1}}\left\{\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})_{(k)}\rVert_{0}=\Theta(n/2^{kp})\right\}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\sum_{k=\log_{2}\tau_{2}}^{\log_{2}\tau_{1}}\exp(\Theta(n/2^{kp}))

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\exp(\Theta(C^{\prime}\log d))=1-\frac{1}{\poly(d)}.

By a union bound over the
d
columns, every large entry level set of every column
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}
has the expected number of elements, up to constant factors, simultaneously with probability at least
99/100
. Conditioned on this event,
\lvert\mathcal{R}_{1}\rvert=O(d\log d)
.

Note that across the
d
columns, there are
\Theta(r/d\log d)
rows corresponding to level sets
k
for
\log_{2}\tau_{2}\leqslant k\leqslant\log_{2}\tau_{1}
. Then with
O(r)
hash buckets, each pair of rows from
\mathcal{R}_{1}\times\mathcal{R}_{2}
is hashed to a separate bucket with probability
O(1/\lvert\mathcal{R}_{1}\times\mathcal{R}_{2}\rvert)=O(1/r)
, so every row in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is uniquely hashed with probability
0.99
by a union bound. Furthermore, with
O(r)=\omega(r/d\log d)
hash buckets, we have by Lemma
2.4
that for each level set
\mathbf{v}_{(k)}
, half of the
\Theta(n/2^{kp})
rows in the
k
th level set get hashed to a bucket with no other row from
\mathcal{R}
with probability at least

1-2\exp\left\lparen\frac{(1/2)^{2}}{12}\Theta(n/2^{kp})\right\rparen=1-\exp\left\lparen\Theta(n/2^{kp})\right\rparen.

Then again by a union bound over the level sets and columns, every large entry level set of every column has at least half of their rows hashed with no other row from
\mathcal{R}
, simultaneously with probability at least
99/100
.

The probability that any two of the large entries lie on the same row is
O(\lvert\mathcal{R}_{2}\rvert^{2}/n)=o(1)
. Then by a union bound, the total success probability for the entire lemma is at least
0.95
.
∎

We apply the lemma above to show that when we write
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{H}+\mathbf{A}^{L}
as in Definition
6.7
, then
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\Omega(n\log(r/d))\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
when we choose
T=nd^{2}\log d/r
.

Lemma 6.12

(Mass of large entries)
.

Let Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{H}+\mathbf{A}^{L}
as in Definition
6.7
with
T=nd^{2}\log d/r
. Let
\mathbf{S}
be a
CountSketch
matrix with
r
rows. Then with probability at least
0.95
,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1})\geqslant\Omega(n\log(r/d^{2}\log d))\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
is the subset of uniquely hashed rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
given by Lemma
6.11
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
be the subset of rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
given by Lemma
6.11
that are hashed to locations without any other rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
. Recall also
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
from the lemma.

We first have that
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1})
since the rows containing entries larger than
\tau_{1}
are perfectly hashed, while rows containing entries between
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{1}
are preserved up to constant factors.

Let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}+\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{>T}
contains the entries of
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
that have absolute value greater than
T
and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}
contains the rest of the entries. Note then that
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{>T}
has at most one nonzero entry per row, and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}
has at most
O(d\cdot r/d\log d)=O(r/\log d)
nonzero entries and thus by Lemma
6.4
,
\lVert\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant O(r)
with probability at least
0.99
. We condition on this event. Then for all
\mathbf{x}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert\lVert\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

Since the
\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{j}
have disjoint support

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert\sum_{k=\log_{2}\tau_{2}}^{\log_{2}\tau_{1}}2^{k}\Theta(n/2^{k})-\lVert\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

Lemmas
6.11
and
6.2

\displaystyle=\Omega(n(\log_{2}\tau_{1}-\log_{2}\tau_{2}))\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-O(r)\lVert\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle=\Omega(n\log(r/d^{2}\log d))\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-O(r^{2})\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\Omega(n\log(r/d^{2}\log d))\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

as desired.
∎

The last thing we need to bound is the mass contribution of the rows of
\mathbf{A}^{L}
that are hashed together with the uniquely hashed rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
. We first bound the columns of the matrix
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}\mathbf{A}^{L}
, where
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
is a subset of hash buckets.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law distribution with index
p\in(0,2)
with cdf
F
. Let
T\coloneqq F^{-1}(1-r/nd^{2}\log d)=\Theta((nd^{2}\log d/r)^{1/p})
,
r^{\prime}<r
. Let
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
be a subset of
r^{\prime}
rows of a
r\times n

CountSketch
matrix
\mathbf{S}
. Let
\mathbf{C}\sim\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(\mathcal{D})^{n\times d}
. Then for each
j\in[d]
,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{S}^{\prime}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen r^{\prime}+\lambda\sqrt{r^{\prime}}\right\rparen(d^{2}\log d)^{1/p-1/2}(n/r)^{1/p}\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{\lambda}.

Proof.

The proof is just a second moment bound and is deferred to Appendix
D
.
∎

Given the above bounds, the rest of the proof is just Hölder’s inequality.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
r\geqslant C(d\log d)^{2}
for a large enough constant
C
. Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{H}+\mathbf{A}^{L}
as in Definition
6.7
with
T=(nd^{2}\log d/r)^{1/p}
. Let
\mathbf{S}
be a
CountSketch
matrix with
r
rows. Let
\mathbf{A}^{L}=\mathbf{C}_{1}+\mathbf{C}_{2}
, where
\mathbf{C}_{1}
is the submatrix formed by the rows of
\mathbf{A}^{L}
that are hashed together with the uniquely hashed rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
by
\mathbf{S}
(c.f. Lemma
6.11
), and
\mathbf{C}_{2}
is the submatrix formed by the rest of the rows. Then with probability at least
0.99
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log d}}\frac{n^{1/p}}{(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1/p-1}}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
be the submatrix of
\mathbf{S}
formed by the set of
r^{\prime}
uniquely hashed rows from Lemma
6.11
, with
r^{\prime}=O(r/d\log d)
. Setting
\lambda=100d
, we have that

r^{\prime}+\lambda\sqrt{r^{\prime}}=O(r^{\prime})=O\left\lparen\frac{r}{d\log d}\right\rparen

so

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen r^{\prime}\right\rparen\lparen d^{2}\log d\rparen^{1/p-1/2}\left\lparen\frac{n}{r}\right\rparen^{1/p}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{S}^{\prime}\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log d}}\frac{n^{1/p}}{(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1/p-1}}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{100d}.

By a union bound over the
d
columns, this is true for all
k\in[d]
with probability at least
0.99
. Conditioned on this event, we have by the triangle inequality that

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{k}\rvert\sum_{i\in S}Y_{i,k}\leqslant O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log d}}\frac{n^{1/p}}{(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1/p-1}}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

as desired.
∎

With the above lemmas in place, we prove Theorem
6.9
.

Proof of Theorem
6.9
.

The “no dilation” bound is just Lemma
6.2
. We thus focus on the “no contraction” bound.

We condition on the results of Lemmas
6.10
,
6.12
, and
6.14
. By a union bound, these all hold simultaneously with probability at least
0.9
. Then, we have for all
\mathbf{x}
that

\frac{1}{\kappa}\geqslant\frac{\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}\geqslant\frac{\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}\geqslant\frac{\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+n\log(r/d^{2}\log d))\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{O(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+n\log(nd^{2}\log d/r))\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}\geqslant\Omega\left\lparen\frac{\log(r/d^{2}\log d)}{\log n}\right\rparen.\qed

6.4
Algorithms for
p\in(1,2)

For power law distributions with index
p\in(1,2)
, we need different algorithms based on the parameter regime: when
n
is rather large, then the distribution looks relatively flat so that sampling is approximately optimal, while when
n
is rather small, then the variance is large enough so that
CountSketch
helps capture and preserve large values that make up a significant fraction of the mass.

6.4.1
Large
n
: sampling

When
n
is large, we shall see that by concentration, sampling alone will give us nearly tight distortion bounds.

We first prove concentration in the upper tail.

Lemma 6.15

(Upper tail concentration)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a power law distribution with index
p\in(1,2)
and let
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n\times d}
. Then,

\ProbOp\left\{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen 1+\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{n^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}n,\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}\geqslant 0.99.

Proof.

By a union bound,
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant B=O((nd)^{1/p})
with probability at least
0.999
. Conditioned on this event, each entry of
\mathbf{A}
is distributed as
\mathsf{trunc}_{B}(\mathcal{D})
. Note that for a random variable
X\sim\mathsf{trunc}_{B}(\mathcal{D})
, we have by Lemma
6.6
that

\displaystyle\E\lvert X\rvert

\displaystyle=\Theta(1)

\displaystyle\E\lvert X\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\Theta(B^{2-p})

Now let
\mathbf{v}\sim\mathsf{trunc}_{B}(\mathcal{D})^{n}
. By the upper tail Bernstein bound,

\ProbOp\left\{\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}-n\E\lvert X\rvert\geqslant\lambda\right\}\leqslant\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{B^{2-p}n+B\lambda/3}\right\rparen.

Then with
\lambda=\kappa n
for

\kappa=\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{n^{1-1/p}},

we have

\lambda=\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{n^{1-1/p}}n=(nd)^{1/p}\log d\geqslant B\log d

and

\lambda^{2}=\frac{d^{2/p}\log^{2}d}{n^{2-2/p}}n^{2}=(nd)^{2/p}\log^{2}d=(nd)^{\frac{1}{p}(2-p)}(nd)\log^{2}d\geqslant B^{2-p}n\log d

so we have that with probability at least
1-1/\poly(d)
,

\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}-n\E\lvert X\rvert\leqslant\lambda\implies\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}\leqslant n\E\lvert X\rvert+\kappa n=\Theta\left\lparen 1+\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{n^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen n.

By a union bound over the
d
columns of
\mathbf{A}
, this holds simultaneously for all columns of
\mathbf{A}
with probability at least
1-1/\poly(d)
. We condition on this event. It then follows that for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\max_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\Theta\left\lparen 1+\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{n^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen n.\qed

To prove concentration in the lower tail, we first need the following lemma.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law distribution with index
p\in(1,2)
. Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}}\left\lparen\lvert\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert\geqslant\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p})\right\rparen=\Omega(1).

(4)

Proof.

The proof is distracting from this discussion and is deferred to Appendix
D
.
∎

Lemma 6.17

(Lower tail concentration)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law distribution with index
p\in(1,2)
. Let
n\geqslant d\log d
. Then,

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n\times d}}\left\{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\Omega(n\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}),\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}\geqslant 0.99.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=1
and let
\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}
. Then by Lemma
6.16
,

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}}\left\lparen\lvert\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert\geqslant\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p})\right\rparen=\Omega(1).

Then by Chernoff bounds, at least
\Omega(n)
of the
n
rows of
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
are at least
\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p})
with probability at least
1-\exp(n)=1-\exp(d\log d)
. We conclude by a standard net argument.
∎

We put the above two parts together for a sketching algorithm based on sampling.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
n\geqslant d\log d
and let
\mathbf{A}
be drawn as an
n\times d
matrix of i.i.d. draws from a
p
-stable distribution. Let

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}

\displaystyle=\Theta\left\lparen 1+\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{n^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\kappa_{r}

\displaystyle=\Theta\left\lparen 1+\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{r^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen

be the distortion upper bound from Lemma
6.15
for when the number of rows is
n
and
r
, respectively. Let
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
be the matrix that samples
r
rows of
\mathbf{A}
, and then scales by
\kappa_{n}d^{1-1/p}n/r
. Then,

\ProbOp\left\{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa_{n}\kappa_{r}d^{2(1-1/p)}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1},\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}\geqslant 0.9

In particular, if

n^{1-1/p}\geqslant d^{1/p}\log d\iff n\geqslant d^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\log^{\frac{p}{p-1}}d,

then

\ProbOp\left\{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen 1+\frac{d^{1/p}}{(r/d^{2})^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen,\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}\geqslant 0.9.

Proof.

By applying lemmas
6.15
and
6.17
, we have that for all
\mathbf{x}
,

\Omega\left\lparen 1\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}n\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa_{n}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}n.

Furthermore, we can apply the lemmas to
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}
as well, which gives us

\Omega(1)\kappa_{n}d^{1-1/p}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}n\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa_{r}\kappa_{n}d^{1-1/p}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}n.

By Hölder’s inequality, we have that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant d^{1-1/p}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}.

Thus,

\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa_{n}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}n\leqslant\kappa_{n}d^{1-1/p}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}n\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

so the sketch does not underestimate norms. On the other hand,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa_{r}\kappa_{n}d^{1-1/p}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}n\leqslant\kappa_{r}\kappa_{n}d^{2(1-1/p)}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}n\leqslant\kappa_{r}\kappa_{n}d^{2(1-1/p)}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

so the sketch does not overestimate norms by more than
\kappa_{r}\kappa_{n}d^{2(1-1/p)}
, as claimed.
∎

6.4.2
Small
n
:
CountSketch

In the previous section, we have handled the case when
n^{1-1/p}\geqslant d^{1/p}\log d
. On the other hand, when
n^{1-1/p}\leqslant d^{1/p}\log d
we will instead use
CountSketch
to hash the largest entries of each column of
\mathbf{A}
. These entries are of size around
n^{1/p}
