, while the entries of vectors with size smaller than this have mass at most
n
. Thus, we approximate the mass up to a factor of

\frac{n}{n^{1/p}}=n^{1-1/p}\leqslant d^{1/p}\log d,

which is roughly what we are shooting for.

Theorem 6.19
.

Let
n^{1-1/p}\leqslant d^{1/p}\log d
. Let
\mathbf{S}
be drawn as a
CountSketch
matrix with
r
rows. Then

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{S},\mathbf{A}}\left\lparen\Omega\left\lparen\frac{1}{\kappa}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1},\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\rparen\geqslant\frac{99}{100}.

where

\kappa=O\left\lparen\left\lparen\frac{n}{(r/d^{2}\log d)}\right\rparen^{1-1/p}\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/p}\log d}{(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen.

Proof.

The distortion upper bound again is just Lemma
6.2
. The distortion lower bound argument is similar to the one presented for the cases of
p\in(0,1]
and thus is deferred to Appendix
D
.
∎

6.5
Algorithms for
p\geqslant 2

When
p\geqslant 2
, we show that any
m\times d
i.i.d. matrix with
m\geqslant\poly(d)
has with constant probability,
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\Theta(m\lVert\mathbf{x}_{2}\rVert)
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This shows that a uniform sampling matrix with
\poly(d)
rows works as a sketch. The following result shows this in expectation.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
p\geqslant 2
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law with index
p
. Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then,

\E_{\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}}\lvert\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert=\Theta(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2})

Proof.

The proof is standard and is deferred to Appendix
D
.
∎

Our strategy then is to show that conditioned on every entry of
\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}
being smaller than some large value
B\geqslant\poly(d)
, the expectation remains approximately unchanged. We then use this in a Bernstein bound to argue the result with high enough probability to union bound over a net.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
p\geqslant 2
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law with index
p
. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/4)
and let
B>\max\{\varepsilon^{-1},(d\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon)^{1/p}\}
. Let
\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}
and fix a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define the events

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}\rvert\leqslant B\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcap_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{E}_{i}

Then for
B
large enough,

\E\left\lparen\lvert\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\geqslant(1-O(\varepsilon))\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert.

Proof.

The proof is standard and is deferred to Appendix
D
.
∎

The following lemma implements the Bernstein bound and applies a standard net argument.

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
p\geqslant 2
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law with index
p
. Let
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{m\times d}
with
m\geqslant\Theta(\max\left\{\varepsilon^{-p},(d^{3/2+1/p}\varepsilon^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1})^{p/(p-1)}\right\})
. For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\mu_{\mathbf{x}}\coloneqq\E_{\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}}\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle
. Then,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))m\mu_{\mathbf{x}},\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\rparen\geqslant 0.95.

Proof.

Note that with probability at least
0.99
,
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{\infty}=O((md)^{1/p})
. Let this event be
\mathcal{E}
. Then conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathbf{A}
is distributed as an i.i.d. matrix drawn from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is the truncation of
\mathcal{D}
at

B=O((md)^{1/p})\geqslant\max\left\{\varepsilon^{-1},(d\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon)^{1/p}\right\}

where the bound on
B
follows by our choice of
m
.

High probability bounds.

Now fix
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By Lemmas
6.20
and
6.21
,

\mu\coloneqq\E\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\E\left\lparen\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen=(1\pm\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{m}\E\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rvert=(1\pm\varepsilon)m\mu_{\mathbf{x}}=\Theta(m\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2})

(5)

and

\sigma^{2}\coloneqq\Var(\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\mid\mathcal{E})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Var(\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rvert\mid\mathcal{E})\leqslant O(m\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}).

Then by Bernstein bounds, we have that

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-\mu\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\mu\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\leqslant 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\varepsilon\mu)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+\varepsilon\mu B/3}\right\rparen\leqslant\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(d\log\varepsilon^{-1})\right\rparen

where the last inequality follows by our choice of
m
. Then chaining together with Equation
5
,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))m\mu_{\mathbf{x}}\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\leqslant\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(d\log\varepsilon^{-1})\right\rparen.

(6)

Net argument.

We now proceed by a standard net argument. Recall the set
\mathcal{S}
and the
\varepsilon
-net
\mathcal{N}
as given in Lemma
2.3
. Now conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
, we have by a union bound that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))m\mu_{\mathbf{x}}
for every
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{N}
, with probability at least
0.99
. We condition on this event as well. Now for any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{S}
, write
\mathbf{y}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{y}^{(i)}
as given in Lemma
2.3
. Then,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant(1+O(\varepsilon))\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{i}\leqslant 1+O(\varepsilon).

We conclude by homogeneity.
∎

Given the above lemma, our subspace embedding follows simply from uniform sampling and rescaling.

Theorem 6.23
.

Let
p\geqslant 2
and let
\mathcal{D}
be a symmetric power law with index
p
. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
, let

r=\Theta(\max\left\{\varepsilon^{-p},(d^{3/2+1/p}\varepsilon^{-1}\log\varepsilon^{-1})^{p/(p-1)}\right\})

and let
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
be a matrix that uniform samples
r
rows and scales by
n/r
. Then,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1},\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\rparen\geqslant 0.9

Proof.

By Lemma
6.22
, we have with probability at least
0.95
that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))n\mu_{\mathbf{x}}

and with probability at least
0.95
that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\frac{n}{r}\cdot(1\pm O(\varepsilon))r\mu_{\mathbf{x}}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))n\mu_{\mathbf{x}}.

Combining these two bounds, we have that with probability at least
0.9
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=(1\pm O(\varepsilon))\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.\qed

6.6
Lower bound

In this section, we work towards proving a lower bound for a general class of random matrices with each column drawn i.i.d. from a different distribution. When specialized to our i.i.d. matrices from the above, we obtain nearly tight bounds. We do not have a single general theorem, but rather a number of different possible arguments that give better bounds depending on the underlying distribution. This can be shown to approximately recover the result of
[
WW19
, Theorem 1.1]
, and additional yields new bounds, for example, the following tight results:

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
\log n\leqslant O(d)
and let
\mathbf{S}
be a
r\times n
matrix such that

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\Cauchy^{n\times d}}\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\right\rparen\geqslant\frac{99}{100}

Then,

\kappa=\Omega\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\log r}\right\rparen

Theorem 6.25
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a
r\times n
matrix such that

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}^{n\times d}}\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\right\rparen\geqslant\frac{99}{100}

where
\mathcal{D}
is a
p
-stable distribution. Then,

\kappa=\Omega\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/p}}{r^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen

Definition 6.26
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{j}
for
j\in[d]
be distributions and consider the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{A}}
over
n\times d
matrices
\mathbf{A}
that draws column
j
from
\mathcal{D}_{j}^{n}
. Let

M_{j}\coloneqq\median_{\mathbf{u}\sim\mathcal{D}_{j}^{n}}\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert_{1}.

We then define the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\max}
that draws entries as

\max_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\lvert v_{j}\rvert}{M_{j}},v_{j}\sim\mathcal{D}_{j}

and let its cdf be
F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}
.

Throughout this section, let
\mathbf{S}
be a
r\times n
matrix such that

\ProbOp_{\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{A}}}\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\kappa\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\delta.

6.6.1
Preliminary bounds on
\mathbf{S}

Lemma 6.27
.

For every
i\in[n]
, we have that

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2\kappa\frac{1+F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)}{F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)}

Proof.

Note that for every row
i
of
\mathbf{A}
, with probability at least
4\delta
, one of the
d
columns of
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, say column
j\in[d]
, has absolute value at least

\frac{\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert}{M_{j}}\geqslant F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)\iff\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert\geqslant F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)M_{j}.

Independently, with probability at least
1/2
, the
\ell_{1}
norm of the rest of the entries of the column is at most

\sum_{i^{\prime}\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i^{\prime}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert\leqslant M_{j}.

Then with probability
2\delta
both of these happen simultaneously, so that

\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\leqslant(1+F^{-1}_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(4\delta))M_{j}

Let this event be
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

Now suppose for contradiction that there is some column
i\in[n]
such that
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert_{1}>2\kappa/F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)
. We then condition on
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Then with probability at least
1/2
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})+\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{i^{\prime}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})\rVert_{1}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen 2\kappa\frac{1+F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)}{F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)}\right\rparen F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)M_{j}=\kappa(1+F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta))M_{j}\geqslant\kappa\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}

so
\mathbf{S}
fails to sketch
\mathbf{A}
with probability at least
\delta
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 6.28
.

Let
F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(4\delta)<x<1
. Then there are at most

\frac{1}{1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(x)}

columns of
\mathbf{S}
with
\ell_{1}
norm more than

2\kappa\frac{1+x}{x}.

Proof.

Let

p\coloneqq\ProbOp_{X\sim\mathcal{D}_{\max}}\left\{X\geqslant x\right\}=1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(x)

and suppose for contradiction that there are more than
1/p
columns of
\mathbf{S}
with
\ell_{1}
norm more than
2\kappa/x
. Note that for each row
i
of these
1/p
rows, there is a
p
probability that one of the
d
columns of
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, say column
j\in[d]
, has absolute value at least

\frac{\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert}{M_{j}}\geqslant x\iff\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert\geqslant xM_{j}.

Then the probability that one of the
1/p
rows, say row
i
, has an entry of absolute value at least
xM_{j}
is at least

1-(1-p)^{1/p}\geqslant 1-e^{-1}.

Independently, with probability at least
1/2
, the
\ell_{1}
norm of the rest of the entries of this column is at most

\sum_{i^{\prime}\in[i]\setminus\{i\}}\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i^{\prime}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert\leqslant M_{j}.

Thus with probability at least
(1-e^{-1})/2
, both of these events happen simultaneously, so there is row
i\in[n]
and a column
j\in[d]
such that

\begin{cases}\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert&\geqslant xM_{j}\\
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}&\leqslant(1+x)M_{j}\\
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert&\geqslant 2\kappa\frac{1+x}{x}\end{cases}.

We condition on this event. Then with probability at least
1/2
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})+\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i^{\prime}}(\mathbf{e}_{i^{\prime}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})\rVert_{1}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{2}2\kappa\frac{1+x}{x}\cdot xM_{j}=\kappa(1+x)M_{j}\geqslant\kappa\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}

so
\mathbf{S}
fails to sketch
\mathbf{A}
with probability at least
(1-e^{-1})/4>\delta
, which is a contradiction.
∎

6.6.2
Distortion lower bound

Fix any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=1
and let
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
. Following
[
WW19
]
, our strategy is to bound
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}
from above in terms of
\kappa
, and then derive a lower bound on
\kappa
by bounding
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}
below by
\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}
.

Note that the bound of Lemma
6.28
is useless when

\frac{1}{1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(x)}\geqslant n\iff F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(x)\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{n}\iff x\geqslant F^{-1}_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}\left\lparen 1-\frac{1}{n}\right\rparen.

We thus set
\mathbf{S}^{H}
to be the matrix formed by taking the columns of
\mathbf{S}
with
\ell_{1}
norm at least

2\kappa\frac{1+F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)}{F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)}

and
\mathbf{S}^{L}
to be the columns of
\mathbf{S}
with
\ell_{1}
norm at most this, and individually bound
\mathbf{S}^{H}\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}
.

Now consider the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{x}}
with cdf
F_{\mathbf{x}}
that draws its entries as
\lvert\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}\rangle\rvert
with
\mathbf{w}\sim\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{D}_{j}
. By a union bound, the largest absolute value entry in
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
is at most
M_{\mathbf{x}}\coloneqq F^{-1}_{\mathbf{x}}(1-1/2n)
with probability at least
1/2
. Let this event be

\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\left\{\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant M_{\mathbf{x}}\right\}.

Throughout this section, we condition on
\mathcal{E}
. We also define

F_{\mathbf{x},\land}(x)\coloneqq\frac{F_{\mathbf{x}}(x)\mathbbm{1}(x\leqslant M_{\mathbf{x}})}{\ProbOp(\mathcal{E})}

to be the conditional cdf of the capped version of
\mathbf{v}
.

6.6.3
Bounding the high-norm columns of
\mathbf{S}

We first bound
\lVert\mathbf{S}^{H}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}
. We will need the following simple lemma:

Lemma 6.29
.

Let
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be vectors with nonnegative entries and unit
\ell_{1}
norm. Let
\mathbf{P}
be a uniformly random permutation matrix. Then,

\E_{\mathbf{P}}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{P}\mathbf{v}\rangle=\frac{1}{n}.

Proof.

We have that

\E_{\mathbf{P}}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{P}\mathbf{v}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\E_{\mathbf{P}}(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{v})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{v_{j}}{n}=\frac{1}{n}.\qed

The main result for this section then is the following:

Lemma 6.30
.

Let

\displaystyle L_{\max}

\displaystyle\coloneqq F^{-1}_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}\left\lparen 1-\frac{1}{n}\right\rparen

\displaystyle L_{\min}

\displaystyle\coloneqq F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}\left\lparen 4\delta\right\rparen

Then,

\ProbOp\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{S}^{H}\frac{\mathbf{v}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant 400\frac{\kappa}{n}\sum_{k=\log_{2}L_{\min}}^{\log_{2}L_{\max}}\frac{1+2^{k-1}}{2^{k}\left\lparen 1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(2^{k})\right\rparen}\right\}\geqslant\frac{99}{100}.

Proof.

Note that i.i.d. distributions are permutation invariant. We first fix the entries of
\mathbf{v}
, which fixes
\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}
, but not the permutation of the entries. Now by Lemma
6.29
, we have

\E_{\mathbf{P}}\left\lVert\mathbf{S}^{H}\mathbf{P}\frac{\mathbf{v}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}}\right\rVert_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{H}\rVert_{1}\E_{\mathbf{P}}\lvert\left\langle\frac{\mathbf{e}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{H}}{\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{H}\rVert_{1}},\mathbf{P}\frac{\mathbf{v}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}}\right\rangle\rvert\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{H}\rVert_{1}\frac{1}{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{H}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}.

By Lemma
6.28
, we have that for each integer
k
between
\log_{2}L_{\min}
and
\log_{2}L_{\max}
, there are at most

\frac{1}{1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(2^{k})}

columns of
\mathbf{S}
with
\ell_{1}
norm more than
2\kappa(1+1/2^{k})
. Thus, there are at most
(1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(2^{k}))^{-1}
columns with
\ell_{1}
norm in
[2\kappa(1+1/2^{k}),2\kappa(1+1/2^{k-1})]
. Then, summing over the bounds over these intervals, we have that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{H}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{k=\log_{2}L_{\min}}^{\log_{2}L_{\max}}2\kappa\left\lparen 1+\frac{1}{2^{k-1}}\right\rparen\frac{1}{1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(2^{k})}=\kappa\sum_{k=\log_{2}L_{\min}}^{\log_{2}L_{\max}}\frac{4(1+2^{k-1})}{2^{k}\left\lparen 1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(2^{k})\right\rparen}.

Chaining together the inequalities gives the bound

\E_{\mathbf{P}}\left\lVert\mathbf{S}^{H}\mathbf{P}\frac{\mathbf{v}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{\kappa}{n}\sum_{k=\log_{2}L_{\min}}^{\log_{2}L_{\max}}\frac{4(1+2^{k-1})}{2^{k}\left\lparen 1-F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(2^{k})\right\rparen}.

We then conclude by Markov’s inequality.
∎

6.6.4
Bounding the low-norm columns of
\mathbf{S}

The idea for bounding
\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}
is that different arguments are needed for different level sets of
\mathbf{v}
, depending on how “spiky” it is. That is, a relatively flat level
\mathbf{v}_{k}
should benefit from the sign cancellations in the product
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}_{k}
, while a very spiky vector such as standard basis vectors should just apply the triangle inequality and bound only the few columns of
\mathbf{S}^{L}
that it touches. This idea is formalized in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.31
.

Let
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
be a fixed matrix such that
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert_{1}\leqslant 1
for each
i\in[n]
, and let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a vector with entries drawn i.i.d. from a distribution with
\E w_{i}=0
and
\sigma^{2}\coloneqq\E w_{i}^{2}<\infty
. Let
\mu\coloneqq\E\lvert w_{i}\rvert
. Then,

\E\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{w}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\min\left\{\mu n,C\sigma\sqrt{rn}\right\}

for an absolute constant
C
.

Proof.

For the first term of the min, we can simply use the triangle inequality to obtain

\E\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{w}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{i}\rVert_{1}\E\lvert w_{i}\rvert=\mu n.

For the second term, we first apply Jensen’s inequality to get

\E\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{w}\rVert_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\E\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{w}\rvert\leqslant C\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{j})^{2}\E w_{j}^{2}\right\rparen^{1/2}=C\sigma\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{2}

for some absolute constant
C
. We then finish by an application of Cauchy-Schwarz, switching from row-wise sums to column-wise sums, and bounding the
\ell_{2}
norm by the
\ell_{1}
norm:

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{r}\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\rparen^{1/2}=\sqrt{r}\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\rparen^{1/2}\leqslant\sqrt{r}\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}^{2}\right\rparen^{1/2}\leqslant\sqrt{rn}.\qed

Now for intuition, in Lemma
6.31
, we roughly think of the distribution of
w_{i}
as being
v_{i}
if
v_{i}
belongs to a level set, and
0
otherwise. Then if
p
is the probability of being in a given level set, the first term is roughly
pn
while the second term is roughly
\sqrt{rpn}
, so the first bound is tighter when
p\leqslant r/n
and the second bound is tighter when
p\geqslant r/n
.

This yields the following:

Corollary 6.32
.

let

T\coloneqq F_{\mathbf{x},\land}^{-1}\left\lparen 1-\frac{r}{n}\right\rparen

and write
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{\leqslant T}+\mathbf{v}_{>T}
, where
\mathbf{v}_{\leqslant T}
takes the value of
\mathbf{v}
on coordinates
i\in[n]
where
\lvert v_{i}\rvert\leqslant T
and
0
otherwise, and
\mathbf{v}_{>T}
similarly takes the coordinates
i\in[n]
of
\mathbf{v}
such that
\lvert v_{i}\rvert>T
and
0
otherwise. Then,
\mathbf{v}_{\leqslant T}
is drawn i.i.d. from a distribution with second moment

\sigma_{\leqslant T}^{2}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{T}x^{2}f_{\mathbf{x},\land}(x)~dx

while
\mathbf{v}_{>T}
is drawn i.i.d. from a distribution with expected absolute value

\mu_{>T}\coloneqq\int_{T}^{M_{\mathbf{x}}}xf_{\mathbf{x},\land}(x)~dx.

Applying Lemma
6.31
, we obtain the bound

\E\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\E\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}_{\leqslant T}\rVert_{1}+\E\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}_{>T}\rVert_{1}\leqslant C\kappa\frac{1+F^{-1}_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(1-1/n)}{F^{-1}_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(1-1/n)}(\sigma_{\leqslant T}\sqrt{rn}+\mu_{>T}n).

6.6.5
Lower bounds for sketching i.i.d.
p
-stable matrices

We apply Corollary
6.32
to prove Theorem
6.24
:

Proof of Theorem
6.25
.

Note that when
\mathbf{A}
is drawn as fully i.i.d. Cauchy variables, then

F^{-1}_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}(1-1/n)=O\left\lparen\frac{nd}{n\log n}\right\rparen=O(d/\log n).

We now apply Corollary
6.32
with
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{1}
, a Cauchy vector. Then,
T=\Theta(n/r)
,
\sigma_{\leqslant T}^{2}=\Theta(n/r)
, and
\mu_{>T}=\Theta(\log r)
which yields

\E\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen\kappa\frac{1+d/\log n}{d/\log n}\left[\sqrt{\frac{n}{r}}\sqrt{rn}+n\log r\right]\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\kappa n\log r\right\rparen.

Then with constant probability, we have

\Omega(n\log n)\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen\kappa n\log r\right\rparen

and thus

\kappa=\Omega\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\log r}\right\rparen,

as desired.
∎

When we have a column drawn i.i.d. from a
p
-stable distribution, we have an alternative bound:

Lemma 6.33
.

Let
\mathbf{v}
be drawn i.i.d. from a
p
-stable distribution for
p\in(1,2)
. If
\mathbf{v}
is in the column space of
\mathbf{S}
, then

2\kappa\frac{1+F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)}{F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)}r^{1-1/p}n^{1/p}=\Omega(n).

Proof.

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\Omega(n)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{v}\rvert=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{L}\rVert_{p}\lvert\mathcal{S}_{i}\rvert

By linearity of expectation, the above sum has expectation
\sum_{i=1}^{r}O\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{L}\rVert_{p}\right\rparen
, and thus is at most a constant times this with probability at least
99/100
by a Markov bound. Then, we proceed by bounding

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{L}\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle\leqslant r^{1-1/p}\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}^{L}\rVert_{p}^{p}\right\rparen^{1/p}

\displaystyle=r^{1-1/p}\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{p}^{p}\right\rparen^{1/p}\leqslant r^{1-1/p}\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}^{p}\right\rparen^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leqslant r^{1-1/p}\left\lparen n\left\lparen 2\kappa\frac{1+F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)}{F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)}\right\rparen^{p}\right\rparen^{1/p}=2\kappa\frac{1+F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)}{F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)}r^{1-1/p}n^{1/p}.

∎

This gives a proof of Theorem
6.25
.

Proof of Theorem
6.25
.

When
\mathbf{A}
is drawn as fully i.i.d.
p
-stable variables, then

F_{\mathcal{D}_{\max}}^{-1}(1-1/n)=\Theta\left\lparen\frac{(nd)^{1/p}}{n}\right\rparen

so by Lemma
6.33
, the distortion bound from these columns is

\Omega(n)\leqslant\kappa\frac{n}{(nd)^{1/p}}r^{1-1/p}n^{1/p}\iff\kappa\geqslant\Omega\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/p}}{r^{1-1/p}}\right\rparen.

∎

Appendix A
Missing proofs from Section
2

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

For each
i\in S
, sample
i
with probability
p
and place the result in a uniformly random hash bucket in
[r]
if it was sampled. Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}
denote the event where
i
is sampled and is hashed to a bucket with no other members of
T
. Let
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{\lvert S\rvert}
denote the sequence of these independent random choices and let
f(C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{s})
denote the number of hash buckets in
[r]
that contains members
i\in S
satisfying
\mathcal{E}_{i}
at the end of the sampling and hashing process. Note that
f
is
1
-Lipschitz, and that

\E f(C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{\lvert S\rvert})=\sum_{i\in S}\ProbOp\left\lparen\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\rparen=\lvert S\rvert p\left\lparen 1-\frac{p}{r}\right\rparen^{\lvert T\rvert}\geqslant p\lvert S\rvert\left\lparen 1-\frac{p\lvert T\rvert}{r}\right\rparen\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)p\lvert S\rvert.

Now consider the Doob martingale

Z_{k}\coloneqq\E\left[f_{q}(C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{\lvert S\rvert})\mid C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{k}\right].

Note that the increments
Z_{k}-Z_{k-1}
conditioned on
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{k-1}
is simply the indicator variable of whether on choice
C_{k}
we sampled an entry and placed it in a new bucket or not. Then
Z_{k}-Z_{k-1}=1
with probability at most
p
and thus
\E_{k-1}(Z_{k}-Z_{k-1})^{2}\leqslant p
. Then by Freedman’s inequality
[
Fre75
]
,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert Z_{\lvert S\rvert}-Z_{0}\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon Z_{0}\right\rparen\leqslant 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)p\lvert S\rvert)^{2}}{p\lvert S\rvert+\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)p\lvert S\rvert/3}\right\rparen\leqslant 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{12}p\lvert S\rvert\right\rparen.\qed

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

We make minor modifications of Lemmas 2.10 and 2.12 in
[
WW19
]
. Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be independent standard Cauchys.

Upper bound.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}\coloneqq\left\{\lvert X_{i}\rvert\leqslant r\log n(\log\log r)^{-1}\right\}
. Then,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\rparen=1-\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan\left\lparen\frac{r\log r}{\log\log r}\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{\log\log r}{r\log r}\gg\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Then,

\E\left\lparen\lvert X_{i}\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen=\E\left\lparen\lvert X_{i}\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\rparen=\frac{1}{\ProbOp(\mathcal{E}_{i})}\frac{1}{\pi}\log\left\lparen 1+\left\lparen\frac{r\log r}{\log\log r}\right\rparen^{2}\right\rparen

and thus by linearity of expectation,

\mu\coloneqq\E\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lvert X_{i}\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen=\frac{1}{\ProbOp(\mathcal{E}_{i})}\frac{r}{\pi}\log\left\lparen 1+\left\lparen\frac{r\log r}{\log\log r}\right\rparen^{2}\right\rparen\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\frac{2}{\pi}r\log r.

Now by a Chernoff bound applied to the
\lvert X_{i}\rvert(\log\log r/r\log r)\in[0,1]
conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mu\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\leqslant\exp\left\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\mu}{3}\frac{\log\log r}{\log r}\right\rparen=\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(\varepsilon^{2}\log\log r)\right\rparen

so

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lvert X_{i}\rvert\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mu\right\rparen

\displaystyle\geqslant\ProbOp\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert X_{i}\rvert\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mu\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\ProbOp(\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle\geqslant\left\lparen 1-\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(\varepsilon^{2}\log\log r)\right\rparen\right\rparen\left\lparen 1-\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{\log\log r}{r\log r}\right\rparen^{r}\geqslant 1-\frac{(3/\varepsilon)^{d}}{\delta}.

Lower bound.

Let
T=\frac{(3/\varepsilon)^{d}}{\delta}
. Note that by Taylor expansion, there is a
T^{\prime}\geqslant 0
such that for
t\geqslant T^{\prime}
,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert X_{i}\rvert>t\right\rparen\geqslant\frac{2}{\pi}t^{-1}+O(t^{-3}).

Now for
i\geqslant 0
and
j\in[r]
, define the indicator

N_{j}^{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\lvert X_{i}\rvert>(1+\varepsilon)^{i}T^{\prime}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and
N^{i}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in[r]}N_{j}^{i}
. Note that by the Taylor expansion bound,

\E N^{i}\geqslant\frac{2r}{\pi}\frac{1}{(1+\varepsilon)^{i}T^{\prime}}.

Then by Chernoff bounds,

\ProbOp\left\lparen N^{i}\geqslant(1+\varepsilon)\E N^{i}\right\rparen\leqslant\exp\left\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\frac{2r}{\pi}\frac{1}{(1+\varepsilon)^{i}T^{\prime}}\right\rparen

Now let
i_{\max}
be the largest
i
such that

\exp\left\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\frac{2r}{\pi}\frac{1}{(1+\varepsilon)^{i}T^{\prime}}\right\rparen\leqslant\frac{1}{T}.

Then by a union bound over the first
i_{\max}
level sets,
N^{i}\geqslant(2/\pi)r(1+\varepsilon)^{i}T^{\prime}
and thus with probability at least
1-1/T
,

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geqslant\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\max}}\frac{2}{\pi}r(1+\varepsilon)^{i}T^{\prime}=\frac{2}{\pi}r\log\left\lparen\frac{1}{T^{\prime}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3}\frac{2r}{\pi}\frac{1}{\log T}\right\rparen\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\frac{2}{\pi}r\log r.

Net argument.

Given the above concentration results, the rest of the argument proceeds as done in
[
WW19
]
, using
1
-stability of Cauchys and then a standard net argument.
∎

Appendix B
No contraction bound

In this section, we prove a no contraction result for a generic
M
-sketch embedding with subsampling rates
p_{h}
as specified in Lemma
B.2
and a hash bucket size of
N_{0}
for the
0
th level and
N
for the
h
th level for
h\in[h_{\max}]
as specified in Definition
B.4
. This allows us to apply the results to both
M
-sketch with random and fixed boundaries, with varied branching factors and failure rates. Recall the definition of the
M
-sketch from Definition
3.7
.

Theorem B.1

(No contraction)
.

Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}=1
. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
\mathbf{S}
be drawn as an
M
-sketch matrix. Then with probability at least
1-6\delta
,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-16\varepsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}.

B.1
Essential weight classes

We first classify a small subset of weight classes of
\mathbf{y}
that we need to preserve for at least a
(1-\varepsilon)
approximation.

Lemma B.2

(Essential weight classes)
.

Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}=1
. Let
m_{\min}
be a minimum class size parameter, let
B
be a branching factor parameter, and let
\varepsilon
be an accuracy parameter. Finally, let
p_{h}=p_{0}/B^{h-1}
for
h\in[\log_{B}n]
be sampling rates. Define

\displaystyle h_{\max}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log_{B}n

\displaystyle q_{\max}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log_{2}\frac{n}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle m_{\min}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{12}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log\frac{4q_{\max}}{\delta}

\displaystyle M_{\geqslant}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log_{2}\frac{B}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle M_{<}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log_{2}\frac{m_{\min}}{p_{0}\varepsilon}

and weight classes

\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{q\in[q_{\max}]:m_{\min}\leqslant p_{h}\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{y})\rvert<Bm_{\min}\right\}

\displaystyle h\in[h_{\max}]

\displaystyle Q_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{q\in\hat{Q}_{h}:q\leqslant M_{\geqslant}+\min_{q\in\hat{Q}_{h}}q,\lVert W_{q}(\mathbf{y})\rVert_{1}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{q_{\max}}\right\}

\displaystyle h\in[h_{\max}]

\displaystyle Q_{<}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{q:\lvert W_{q}(\mathbf{y})\rvert<m_{\min}/p_{0},q\leqslant M_{<},\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{M_{<}}\right\}

\displaystyle Q^{*}

\displaystyle\coloneqq Q_{<}\cup\bigcup_{h\in[h_{\max}]}Q_{h}

Then,

\sum_{q\in Q^{*}}\lVert W_{q}(\mathbf{y})\rVert_{1}\geqslant 1-6\varepsilon

Remark B.3
.

The
\hat{Q}_{h}
are the weight classes for which the
h
th level is the smallest level at which we sample at least
m_{\min}
elements of
W_{q}
in expectation, so that the mass is extremely concentrated. The
Q_{h}
are the weight classes that restrict
\hat{Q}_{h}
to only as many levels as we need to preserve the mass of
\hat{Q}_{h}
up to a
1-\varepsilon
factor. The set
Q_{<}
specifies the subset of levels that are too small for concentration, but are needed to preserve the mass of
\mathbf{y}
up to a
1-\varepsilon
factor. The set
Q^{*}
specifies the union of these essential weight classes needed for a
1-\varepsilon
approximation.

Proof.

Note that

\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{n}\sum_{q>q_{\max}}\lvert W_{q}\rvert_{1}\leqslant\varepsilon

so we restrict our attention to
q\in[q_{\max}]
. Note that every
q\in[q_{\max}]
belongs in either exactly one class
\hat{Q}_{h}
, or
\lvert W\rvert_{q}<m_{\min}/p_{0}
. The total weight of weight classes with
\lvert W_{q}\rvert<m_{\min}/p_{0}
and
q>M_{<}
is at most

\sum_{q>M_{<}}\lvert W_{q}\rvert 2^{1-q}=2\frac{m_{\min}}{p_{0}}2^{-M_{<}}\sum_{q>0}2^{-q}\leqslant 2\frac{m_{\min}}{p_{0}}\frac{p_{0}\varepsilon}{m_{\min}}=2\varepsilon.

Furthermore, let
h\in[h_{\max}]
and let
q_{h}^{*}\coloneqq\min_{q\in\hat{Q}_{h}}q
. Then the ratio of the total weight of classes in
W_{q}
with
q>M_{\geqslant}+q_{h}^{*}
to
\lVert W_{q_{h}^{*}}\rVert_{1}
is at most

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lVert W_{q_{h}^{*}}\rVert_{1}}\sum_{q>M_{\geqslant}+q_{h}^{*}}2^{1-q}\frac{Bm_{\min}}{p_{h}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2^{-q_{h}^{*}}m_{\min}/p_{h}}\sum_{q>M_{\geqslant}+q_{h}^{*}}2^{1-q}\frac{Bm_{\min}}{p_{h}}

\displaystyle=2B\sum_{q>M_{\geqslant}}2^{-q}\leqslant 2B2^{-M_{\geqslant}}\leqslant 2B\frac{\varepsilon}{B}=2\varepsilon.

We thus have that

\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}\sum_{q>M_{\geqslant}+q_{h}^{*}}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{h\in[h_{\max}]}2\varepsilon\lVert W_{q_{h}^{*}}\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2\varepsilon.

Furthermore, the total weight of classes in
W_{q}
with
\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}<\varepsilon/q_{\max}
is at most

\sum_{q:\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}<\varepsilon/q_{\max}}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\leqslant q_{\max}\frac{\varepsilon}{q_{\max}}=\varepsilon.

We conclude by combining the above bounds.
∎

B.2
Approximate perfect hashing

Definition B.4

(Useful constants)
.

\displaystyle N_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{\delta}\frac{M_{<}}{p_{0}\varepsilon}m_{\min}\left\lparen 1+\frac{7}{6}\frac{2\log(2M_{<}/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle N_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2N_{0}^{\prime}\log N_{0}^{\prime}

Number of hash buckets at the
0
th level

\displaystyle N^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{B}{\varepsilon}m_{\min}\left\lparen M_{\geqslant}+\frac{7}{6}\frac{2q_{\max}\log(2q_{\max}/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2N^{\prime}\log N^{\prime}

Number of hash buckets

We allow the flexibility to choose the number of buckets
N_{0}
and
N
to be larger if needed. The
N_{0}
and
N
are chosen so that

\frac{M_{<}}{p_{0}}m_{\min}\left\lparen 1+\frac{7}{6}\frac{2\log(2N_{0}M_{<}/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen\leqslant\delta\varepsilon N_{0}

and

Bm_{\min}\left\lparen M_{\geqslant}+\frac{7}{6}\frac{2q_{\max}\log(2Nq_{\max}/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen\leqslant\varepsilon N.

Lemma B.5

(Concentration of sampled mass)
.

Suppose
p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\geqslant m_{\min}
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta/q_{\max}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}}b_{i,h}

\displaystyle=(1\pm\varepsilon)p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}}\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert b_{i,h}

\displaystyle=(1\pm\varepsilon)p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}

Proof.

Let

X\coloneqq\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}}b_{i,h}.

By the Chernoff bound,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert X-\E X\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\E X\right\rparen\leqslant 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\E X}{3}\right\rparen\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2q_{\max}}.

Similarly, let

Y\coloneqq\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}}\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert b_{i,h}.

Note that

\displaystyle\E Y

\displaystyle=p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\geqslant 2^{-q}p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert b_{i,h}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{1-q}

\displaystyle\Var(\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert b_{i,h})

\displaystyle\leqslant p_{h}2^{2-2q}

so by Bernstein’s inequality,

\displaystyle\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert Y-\E Y\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon\E Y\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\varepsilon\E Y)^{2}}{p_{h}2^{2-2q}\lvert W_{q}\rvert+(\varepsilon\E Y)2^{1-q}/3}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\varepsilon 2^{-q}p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert)^{2}}{p_{h}2^{2-2q}\lvert W_{q}\rvert+(\varepsilon 2^{1-q}p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert)2^{1-q}/3}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{8}\frac{p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\varepsilon^{2}}{1+\varepsilon/3}\right\rparen\leqslant 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{12}p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\right\rparen\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2q_{\max}}.

We conclude by a union bound over the two events.
∎

The following lemma uses a standard balls and bins martingale argument (e.g.,
[
Lee16
]
) to show that most items are hashed uniquely.

Lemma B.6

(Approximately perfect hashing)
.

Let
h\in[h_{\max}]
and let
Q\subseteq\left\{q:p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\geqslant m_{\min}\right\}
. Let
\hat{W}\subset\mathbf{y}
contain
W_{Q}\coloneqq\bigcup_{q\in Q}W_{q}
. Let
p_{h}\lvert\hat{W}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon N
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
. Then with probability at least
1-(3/2)\lvert Q\rvert\delta/q_{\max}
, every
W_{q}
has a
W_{q}^{*}\subset W_{q}
that gets sampled and placed in a hash bucket with no other members of
\hat{W}
, and
\lvert W_{q}^{*}\rvert\geqslant(1-3\varepsilon)p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert
and
\lVert W_{q}^{*}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-9\varepsilon)p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
2.4
to see that with probability at least

1-2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{12}p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\right\rparen\leqslant 1-\frac{\delta}{2q_{\max}},

there is a set
W_{q}^{*}\subseteq W_{q}
of elements that are hashed to a bucket with no other element of
\hat{W}
in it and of size
\lvert W_{q}^{*}\rvert\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{2}p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\geqslant(1-3\varepsilon)p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert
with probability at least
1-\delta/2q_{\max}
. We condition on this event.

By Lemma
B.5
, with probability at least
1-\delta/q_{\max}
, we sample
(1\pm\varepsilon)p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert
elements with mass
(1\pm\varepsilon)p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}
. Note then that there are at most
4\varepsilon p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert
sampled elements that do not belong
W_{q}^{*}
. The mass of these elements is at most

4\varepsilon p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert 2^{1-q}\leqslant 8\varepsilon p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}.

Thus,

\lVert W_{q}^{*}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}-8\varepsilon p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}=(1-9\varepsilon)p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}.

We conclude by a union bound over the weight classes
Q
.
∎

B.3
Preserving weight classes

Definition B.7
.

\displaystyle\tau_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{p_{0}\varepsilon}{2M_{<}m_{\min}}

Size of a relatively large element at
0
th level

\displaystyle T_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{6}{7}\frac{\varepsilon\tau_{0}}{\log(2N_{0}M_{<}/\delta)}

Size of a relatively small element at
0
th level

\displaystyle\tau_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{p_{h}\varepsilon}{2q_{\max}Bm_{\min}}

Size of a relatively large element

\displaystyle T_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{6}{7}\frac{\varepsilon\tau_{h}}{\log(2Nq_{\max}/\delta)}

Size of a relatively small element

Definition B.8

(Large elements)
.

\displaystyle Q_{<,0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{q:q\leqslant\log_{2}\frac{1}{T_{0}}\right\}

\displaystyle Q_{<,h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{q:q\leqslant\log_{2}\frac{1}{T_{h}}\right\}

The weight class
Q_{<,h}
is the set of relatively large elements at the
h
th level of sampling.

We directly recall the following Lemma 3.3 from
[
CW15
]
.

Lemma B.9
.

Let
h\in[h_{\max}]
,
\bar{W}\subset\mathbf{y}
,
T\geqslant\lVert\bar{W}\rVert_{\infty}
, and
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
. If

N\geqslant\frac{6\lVert\bar{W}\rVert_{1}}{T\log(N/\delta)},

then

\ProbOp\left\lparen\max_{k\in[N]}\lVert L_{h,k}\cap\bar{W}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{7}{6}T\log(N/\delta)\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\delta^{\prime}.

B.3.1
Preserving weight classes in
Q_{h}

Lemma B.10
.

Let
h\in[h_{\max}]
,
q\in Q_{h}
. Then

\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert\geqslant\tau_{h}=\frac{p_{h}\varepsilon}{2q_{\max}Bm_{\min}}.

Proof.

By the definition of
Q_{h}
, we have that
m_{\min}\leqslant p_{h}\lvert W_{q}\rvert\leqslant Bm_{\min}
and
\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{q_{\max}}
. We then have that

\lvert W_{q}\rvert 2^{1-q}\geqslant\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{q_{\max}}.

Then for any
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}
,

\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert\geqslant 2^{-q}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2\lvert W_{q}\rvert q_{\max}}\geqslant\frac{p_{h}\varepsilon}{2q_{\max}Bm_{\min}}.\qed

Lemma B.11
.

Let
h\in[h_{\max}]
and let
L_{h}(\mathbf{y}_{i})
denote the multiset of elements in the hash bucket in the
h
th level containing
\mathbf{y}_{i}
. Then with probability at least
1-2\lvert Q_{h}\rvert\delta/q_{\max}
, for all
q\in Q_{h}
, we sample a set
W_{q}^{*}\subseteq W_{q}
such that

\lVert W_{q}^{*}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-9\varepsilon)p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}

and for every
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}^{*}
,

\left\lvert\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{j}\in L_{h}(\mathbf{y}_{i})}\Lambda_{j}\mathbf{y}_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert.

Proof.

Let
\hat{W}=W_{Q_{h}}\cup W_{Q_{<,h}}
. Then by our choice of
N
,

\lvert\hat{W}\rvert\leqslant\lvert W_{Q_{h}}\rvert+\lvert W_{Q_{<,h}}\rvert\leqslant M_{\geqslant}\frac{Bm_{\min}}{p_{h}}+\frac{1}{T_{h}}=\frac{B}{p_{h}}m_{\min}\left\lparen M_{\geqslant}+\frac{7}{6}\frac{2q_{\max}\log(2Nq_{\max}/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rparen\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon N}{p_{h}}.

Then by Lemma
B.6
, with probability at least
1-(3/2)\lvert Q_{h}\rvert/q_{\max}
, for each
q\in Q_{h}
, there is a set of sampled elements
W_{q}^{*}\subseteq W_{q}
that get hashed to a bucket with no other members of
\hat{W}
, and
\lVert W_{q}^{*}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-9\varepsilon)p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}
.

Note that for each
q\in Q_{h}
and
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}^{*}
, the absolute value of the largest element in
L_{h}(\mathbf{y}_{i})
not equal to
\mathbf{y}_{i}
is at most
T_{h}
, since we have hashed the elements of
W_{Q_{<,h}}
to other buckets. Then by Lemma
B.9
, the
\ell_{1}
mass of elements that are at most
T_{h}
in all hash buckets are at most

\lVert L_{h}(\mathbf{y}_{i})\setminus\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{7}{6}T_{h}\log(2Nq_{\max}/\delta)=\varepsilon\tau_{h}

with probability at least
1-\delta/2q_{\max}
. By a union bound over
q\in Q_{h}
, this is true for all
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}^{*}
for
q\in Q_{h}
with probability at least
1-2\lvert Q_{h}\rvert\delta/q_{\max}
.

Recall from Lemma
B.10
that
\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert\geqslant\tau_{h}
for all
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}
with
q\in Q_{h}
. Note then that the mass of this hash bucket is at least

\left\lvert\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{j}\in L_{h}(\mathbf{y}_{i})}\Lambda_{j}\mathbf{y}_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert-\lVert L_{h}(\mathbf{y}_{i})\setminus\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert-\varepsilon\tau_{h}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert

which is the desired bound. Thus overall, the total success probability is at least
1-2\lvert Q_{h}\rvert\delta/q_{\max}
.
∎

Lemma B.12
.

Let
h\in[h_{\max}]
. Then with probability at least
1-2\lvert Q_{h}\rvert\delta/q_{\max}
, we have that

\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-10\varepsilon)\sum_{q\in Q_{h}}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}.

Proof.

Taking a sum over
q\in Q_{h}
and
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}^{*}
, we find that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(h)}\mathbf{S}^{(h)}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{p_{h}}\sum_{q\in Q_{h}}\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}}b_{i,h}\left\lvert\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{j}\in L_{h}(\mathbf{y}_{i})}\Lambda_{j}\mathbf{y}_{j}\right\rvert

Looking only at rows in
Q_{h}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{p_{h}}\sum_{q\in Q_{h}}\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}^{*}}\left\lvert\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{j}\in L_{h}(\mathbf{y}_{i})}\Lambda_{j}\mathbf{y}_{j}\right\rvert

Looking only at good sampled elements
W_{q}^{*}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{p_{h}}\sum_{q\in Q_{h}}\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}^{*}}(1-\varepsilon)\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert

Lemma
B.11

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{p_{h}}\sum_{q\in Q_{h}}\lVert W_{q}^{*}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)(1-9\varepsilon)\frac{1}{p_{h}}\sum_{q\in Q_{h}}p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}

Lemma
B.11

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-10\varepsilon)\sum_{q\in Q_{h}}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}

which is the desired bound. The failure probability is the same as from Lemma
B.11
.
∎

B.3.2
Preserving weight classes in
Q_{<}

With essentially the exact same proofs as in the above section, we have the following analogues of Lemmas
B.10
,
B.11
, and
B.12
.

Lemma B.13
.

Let
q\in Q_{<}
. Then

\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert\geqslant\tau_{0}=\frac{\varepsilon p_{0}}{M_{<}m_{\min}}.

Lemma B.14
.

Let
Q=\left\{q\in Q_{<}:\lvert W_{q}\rvert\geqslant m_{\min}\right\}
. Let
L_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{i})
denote the multiset of elements in the hash bucket in the
0
th level containing
\mathbf{y}_{i}
. Then with probability at least
1-2\lvert Q\rvert\delta/M_{<}
, for all
q\in Q
, there is a set
W_{q}^{*}\subseteq W_{q}
such that
W_{q}^{*}
is hashed to a different bucket than
W_{Q_{<,0}}\supset W_{Q_{<}}
,

\lVert W_{q}^{*}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-9\varepsilon)p_{h}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1},

and for every
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{q}^{*}
,

\left\lvert\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{j}\in L_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{i})}\Lambda_{j}\mathbf{y}_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert.

Lemma B.15
.

Let
Q=\left\{q\in Q_{<}:\lvert W_{q}\rvert\geqslant m_{\min}\right\}
. Then with probability at least
1-2\lvert Q\rvert\delta/M_{<}
,

\lVert\mathbf{C}^{(0)}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-10\varepsilon)\sum_{q\in Q}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}.

It thus remains to handle the case of
\{q\in Q_{<}:\lvert W_{q}\rvert<m_{\min}\}
. For these small level sets, we can perfectly hash these into separate buckets from all the entries in
Q_{<,0}
.

Lemma B.16
.

Let
Q=\{q\in Q_{<}:\lvert W_{q}\rvert<m_{\min}\}
. Let
L_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{i})
denote the multiset of elements in the hash bucket in the
0
th level containing
\mathbf{y}_{i}
. With probability at least
1-2\delta
, every member of
W_{Q}
is hashed to a different bucket than
W_{Q_{<,0}}\supset W_{Q_{<}}
, and we have for every
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{Q}
that

\left\lvert\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{j}\in L_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{i})}\Lambda_{j}\mathbf{y}_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert.

Proof.

Note that

N_{0}\geqslant\frac{1}{\delta}\lvert W_{Q}\rvert\lvert W_{Q_{<,0}}\rvert.

Then for every
(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})\in W_{Q}\times W_{Q_{<,0}}
, there is a
\delta/\lvert W_{Q}\rvert\lvert W_{Q_{<,0}}\rvert
probability that
\mathbf{y}_{i}
and
\mathbf{y}_{j}
get hashed to the same location. By a union bound, none of these pairs are hashed to to the same location with probability at least
1-\delta
. Then by Lemma
B.9
, the
\ell_{1}
mass of elements that are at most
T_{0}
in all hash buckets are at most

\lVert L_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{i})\setminus\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{7}{6}T_{0}\log(2N_{0}M_{<}/\delta)=\varepsilon\tau_{0}

with probability at least
1-\delta/2M_{<}
. By a union bound over
q\in Q
, this is true for all
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in W_{Q}
with probability at least
1-\lvert Q\rvert\delta/2M_{<}\geqslant 1-\delta
. Then,

\left\lvert\sum_{\mathbf{y}_{j}\in L_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{i})}\Lambda_{j}\mathbf{y}_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert-\lVert L_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{i})\setminus\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert-\varepsilon\tau_{0}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\rvert

which is the desired bound. Thus overall, the failure probability is
1-2\delta
.
∎

B.4
Proof of Theorem
B.1

We finally gather the pieces from above.

Proof.

Proof of Theorem
B.1

We union bound over the events and sum over the results of Lemmas
B.15
,
B.16
, and
B.12
, so that with probability at least
1-6\delta
,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}\rVert_{1}\geqslant(1-10\varepsilon)\sum_{q\in Q_{<}\cup\bigcup_{h\in[h_{\max}]}Q_{h}}\lVert W_{q}\rVert_{1}.

We conclude by chaining this inequality together with the result of Lemma
B.2
.
∎

Appendix C
Missing proofs from Section
4

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

By Hoeffding bounds, we have for each
j\in[d]
that

\ProbOp\left\lparen\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{s}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{i}\right\rvert>\sqrt{\frac{s}{2}\log\frac{2d}{\delta}}\right\rparen\leqslant 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{2(\sqrt{(s/2)\log(2d/\delta)})^{2}}{s}\right\rparen\leqslant\frac{\delta}{d}

Then by a union bound over the
d
choices of
j
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the complement event of the above holds for every
j\in[d]
. Conditioned on this event, we have that

\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{s}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{s}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{i}\right\rvert\leqslant d\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{2d}{\delta}}\sqrt{s}

as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

For each
i\in[r]
and
j\in[d]
, by the
1
-stability of Cauchy variables,

\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\mathcal{C}_{i,j}

where
\mathcal{C}_{i,j}
are drawn as standard Cauchy variables, and are independent for distinct
j
. Now note that
\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert\leqslant O(rd)
with probability at least
1-(100rd)^{-1}
and thus by a union bound,
\max_{i\in[r],j\in[d]}\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert\leqslant O(rd)
with probability at least
1-1/400
. We condition on this event. Note then that the conditional expectation is at most

\E\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert\leqslant O(\log(rd))

as shown in
[
Ind06b
]
. Then,

\E\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\E\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rvert=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\E\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert=O(d\log(rd))\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}

so a Markov bound and a union bound with the earlier event shows that

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(d\log(rd))\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{200}.

For the lower bound, let
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}
be the truncation of
\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}
at
d
, i.e.,

\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{C}_{i,j}&\text{if $\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert\leqslant d$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Note then that by
[
Ind06b
, Lemma 6]
,
\Var(\lvert\hat{C}_{i,j}\rvert)=\Theta(d)
so

\sigma^{2}\coloneqq\Var\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\lvert\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}\rvert\right\rparen=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Theta(d)\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}^{2}=\Theta(d^{2})\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}^{2}

and

\mu\coloneqq\E\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\lvert\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}\rvert\right\rparen=\Theta(d\log d)\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}.

Then by Chebyshev’s inequality,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\lvert\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}\rvert-\mu\leqslant\Theta(\log d)\sigma\right\rparen\leqslant\frac{1}{400}.

Thus, with probability at least
1-1/400
,

\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert\geqslant\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\lvert\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{i,j}\rvert\geqslant\gamma\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}

for
\gamma=\Omega(d\log d)
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}
denote the above event, so that
\ProbOp(\mathcal{E}_{i})\geqslant 1-1/400
. Then,

\E\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathbbm{1}(\neg\mathcal{E}_{i})\left[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert\right]\right\rparen\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1}{400}\gamma\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}

so by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-1/200
,

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathbbm{1}(\neg\mathcal{E}_{i})\left[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert\right]\leqslant\frac{\gamma}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}=\frac{\gamma}{2}\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}.

Then, conditioning on this event,

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}\lvert\mathcal{C}_{i,j}\rvert\right](1-\mathbbm{1}(\neg\mathcal{E}_{i}))\geqslant\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}=\frac{\gamma}{2}\lVert\mathbf{S}\rVert_{1}

as desired.
∎

Appendix D
Missing proofs from Section
6

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Because
\ProbOp(\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T)=\Theta(1)
for
T
large enough,

\E_{X\sim\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(\mathcal{D})}\lvert X\rvert=\Theta(1)\E_{X\sim\mathcal{D}}(\lvert X\rvert\mid\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T),\qquad\E_{X\sim\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(\mathcal{D})}X^{2}=\Theta(1)\E_{X\sim\mathcal{D}}(X^{2}\mid\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T)

By the layer cake theorem,

\displaystyle\E_{X\sim\mathcal{D}}(\lvert X\rvert\mid\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert>x\mid\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T\right\rparen~dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\ProbOp\left\lparen x<\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T\right\rparen}{\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T\right\rparen}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T\right\rparen}\int_{0}^{T}\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert>x\right\rparen-\ProbOp(\lvert X\rvert>T)~dx

\displaystyle=\Theta(1)\int_{0}^{T}\Theta(x^{-p})~dx

and similarly,

\E_{X\sim\mathcal{D}}(X^{2}\mid\lvert X\rvert\leqslant T)=\Theta(1)\int_{0}^{T}x\ProbOp(\lvert X\rvert>x)~dx=\Theta(1)\int_{0}^{T}\Theta(x^{1-p})~dx.

Solving the simple integrals yields the desired results.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.8
.

The distortion upper bound is just Lemma
6.2
.

Mass of small entries.

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{H}+\mathbf{A}^{L}
as in Definition
6.7
, with
T=O\left\lparen(nd^{2}\log d/r)^{1/p}\right\rparen
. Then,
\mathbf{A}^{L}\sim\mathsf{trunc}_{T}(\mathcal{D})^{n\times d}
where by Lemma
6.6
, the first two moments of each entry are

\mu=\Theta(T^{1-p}),\qquad\sigma=\Theta(T^{2-p}).

Then by Bernstein’s inequality,

\displaystyle-\log\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\geqslant 2\mu n\right\rparen

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\mu n)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}n+\mu nT/3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega((T^{1-p}n)^{2})}{O(T^{2-p})n+O(T^{1-p})nT}=\Omega(nT^{-p})=\Omega\left\lparen\frac{r}{d^{2}\log d}\right\rparen=\Omega(\log d)

Thus,
\ProbOp(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2\mu n)\geqslant 1-1/\poly(d)
so by a union bound over the
d
columns, this event simultaneously holds for all
d
columns with probability at least
1-1/\poly(d)
. Conditioned on this event, by the triangle inequality,

\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\leqslant O\left\lparen\frac{n^{1/p}}{(\log d)^{1/p-1}}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Mass of large entries.

Furthermore, let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
be the subset of rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
given by Lemma
6.11
that are hashed to locations without any other rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
. Recall also
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
from the lemma.

We first have that
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1})
since the rows containing entries larger than
\tau_{1}
are perfectly hashed, while rows containing entries between
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{1}
are preserved up to constant factors.

Let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}+\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{>T}
contains the entries of
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
that have absolute value greater than
T
and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}
contains the rest of the entries. Note then that
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{>T}
has at most one nonzero entry per row, and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}
has at most
O(d\cdot r/d\log d)=O(r/\log d)
nonzero entries and thus by Lemma
6.4
,
\lVert\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant O(r^{1/p})
with probability at least
0.99
. We condition on this event. Then for all
\mathbf{x}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert\lVert\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{j}
have disjoint support

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert\sum_{k=\log_{2}\tau_{2}}^{\log_{2}\tau_{1}}2^{k}\Theta(n/2^{kp})-\lVert\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

Lemmas
6.11
and
6.2

\displaystyle=\Omega((n/\log d)^{1/p}\log d)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-O(r)\lVert\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle=\Omega(n^{1/p}/(\log d)^{1/p-1})\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-O(r^{1+1/p})\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\Omega(n^{1/p}/(\log d)^{1/p-1})\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

Conclusion.

On the other hand, by Lemma
6.14
, the mass of the
O(r/d\log d)
rows that are hashed together with the rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
have mass at most

O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log d}}\frac{n^{1/p}}{(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1/p-1}}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=o\left\lparen n^{1/p}/(\log d)^{1/p-1}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

Then,

\frac{1}{\kappa}\geqslant\frac{\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}\geqslant\frac{\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}\geqslant\frac{\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+n^{1/p}/(\log d)^{1/p-1})\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{O(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+n^{1/p}/(\log d)^{1/p-1}))\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}\geqslant\Omega\left\lparen 1\right\rparen.\qed

Proof of Lemma
6.13
.

For a hash bucket
i\in[r]
and
k\in[d]
, let

Y_{i,k}\coloneqq\left\lvert\sum_{j:h(j)=i}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{e}_{k}\right\rvert

where
h
is the hash function for the
CountSketch
matrix
\mathbf{S}
. By Chernoff bounds and a union bound, there are
\Theta(n/r)
rows
j\in[n]
such that
h(j)=i
for all buckets
i\in[r]
, with probability at least
1-r\exp(-\Theta(n/r))=1-o(1)
. Conditioned on this event, which is independent of the randomness of
\mathbf{C}
,

\displaystyle\E Y_{i,k}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}\in h^{-1}(i)\times h^{-1}(i)}\E\left[(\mathbf{e}_{j_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{e}_{k})(\mathbf{e}_{j_{2}}^{\top}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{e}_{k})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j:h(j)=i}\E\left\lparen\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{e}_{k}\right\rparen^{2}=O\left\lparen\frac{n}{r}T^{2-p}\right\rparen=O\left\lparen(d^{2}\log d)^{(2-p)/p}\left\lparen\frac{n}{r}\right\rparen^{2/p}\right\rparen

by the second moment bound in Lemma
6.6
.

Now let
S
be the subset of rows of
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
. Then for each
k\in[d]
,

\displaystyle\E\left[\sum_{i\in S}Y_{i,k}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S}\E Y_{i,k}\leqslant\sum_{i\in S}\sqrt{\E Y_{i,k}^{2}}=O\left\lparen r^{\prime}(d^{2}\log d)^{1/p-1/2}(n/r)^{1/p}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\Var\left\lparen\sum_{i\in S}Y_{i,k}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S}\Var(Y_{i,k})=O\left\lparen r^{\prime}(d^{2}\log d)^{(2-p)/p}\left\lparen n/r\right\rparen^{2/p}\right\rparen.

By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\sum_{i\in S}Y_{i,k}\leqslant\E\left[\sum_{i\in S}Y_{i,k}\right]+\lambda\sqrt{\Var\left\lparen\sum_{i\in S}Y_{i,k}\right\rparen}\right\rparen\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{\lambda}

which gives the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.16
.

We compare
\mathcal{D}
to a
p
-stable distribution
\mathcal{D}_{p}
. By
[
Nol18
, Theorem 1.12]
, a
p
-stable distribution is a power law with index
p
. Then, there exist constants
T
and
c
such that for all
t\geqslant T
,

\ProbOp_{X\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}}\left\lparen cX>t\right\rparen\leqslant\ProbOp_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}}\left\lparen Y>t\right\rparen.

We then define the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\prime}
which draws
Z\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\prime}
as
cX
for
X\sim\mathcal{D}
if
\lvert cX\rvert>T
, and
0
otherwise. Note then that for
Z\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\prime}
and
Y\sim\mathcal{D}
,
\lvert Y\rvert
stochastically dominates
\lvert Z\rvert
.

We are then in the position to apply the following theorem from probability theory.

Theorem D.1

(Theorem 2,
[
Pru97
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{d}
be independent symmetric random variables, and suppose
Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{d}
are also independent symmetric random variables. Assume that for every
j
we have
\lvert Y_{j}\rvert
stochastically dominated by
\lvert X_{j}\rvert
. Then

\ProbOp\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}Y_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant\lambda\right\}\leqslant 2\ProbOp\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}X_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant\lambda\right\}

for every positive
\lambda
.

Thus, it suffices to show Equation
4
for
\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\prime}
in place of
\mathcal{D}
. For
j\in[d]
, let
X_{j}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}
and define

\hat{X}_{j}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\lvert cX_{j}\rvert>T$}\\
X_{j}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Note then that
X_{j}-\hat{X}_{j}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\prime}
, so

\ProbOp\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}Y_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant\lambda\right\}=\ProbOp\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}(X_{j}-X_{j}^{\prime})\right\rvert\geqslant\lambda\right\}.

We first have by
p
-stability that

\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}X_{j}\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\lvert\hat{X}\rvert

for a
p
-stable variable
\hat{X}
, so there are constants
R
,
p
such that

\ProbOp\left\lparen\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}X_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant R\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\right\rparen=\ProbOp\left\lparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\lvert\hat{X}\rvert\geqslant R\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\right\rparen=\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert\hat{X}\rvert\geqslant R\right\rparen\geqslant p.

Next note that
X_{j}^{\prime}\leqslant T/c=O(1)
so

\E\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}X_{j}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leqslant\sqrt{\E\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}X_{j}^{\prime}\right\rvert^{2}}=\E\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}\E[X_{i}^{\prime}X_{j}^{\prime}]}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{2}\E X_{j}^{\prime 2}}=O(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2})

by Jensen’s inequality. Then by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-p/2
,
\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}X_{j}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leqslant C\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}
for some constant
C
that depends on
p
. Then for
\mathbf{x}
such that
R\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\geqslant 2C\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}
, we have by a union bound that

\ProbOp\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}(X_{j}-X_{j}^{\prime})\right\rvert\geqslant\frac{R}{2}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\right\}\geqslant\ProbOp\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}X_{j}\right\rvert-\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}X_{j}^{\prime}\right\rvert\geqslant\frac{R}{2}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\right\}\geqslant\frac{p}{2}.

On the other hand, if
R\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}<2C\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}
, the argument in Lemma
6.20
shows that

\ProbOp\left\lparen\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}Y_{j}\right\rvert\geqslant\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2})\right\rparen=\Omega(1)

so the result holds under this case as well.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.19
.

The distortion upper bound is just Lemma
6.2
.

Mass of small entries.

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{H}+\mathbf{A}^{L}
as in Definition
6.7
, with
T=O\left\lparen(nd^{2}\log d/r)^{1/p}\right\rparen
.

By Lemma
6.3
, the sizes and mass of all level sets
\mathbf{v}_{(k)}
with entries at most
2^{k}\leqslant T
are concentrated around their means up to constant factors with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Theta(n2^{-kp}))
. Thus by a union bound over
d
columns
j
and level sets
0\leqslant k\leqslant\log_{2}T
, with probability at least

1-d\sum_{k=0}^{\log_{2}T}\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(n2^{-kp})\right\rparen\geqslant 1-d\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(\log d)\right\rparen=1-\frac{1}{\poly(r/d^{2}\log d)}

we have for all
j\in[d]
and
0\leqslant k\leqslant\log_{2}T
that

\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})_{(k)}\rVert_{0}=\Theta(n2^{-kp})\qquad\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})_{(k)}\rVert_{1}=\Theta(n2^{k(1-p)})

Then

\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{\log_{2}T}\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{j})_{(k)}\rVert_{1}=O(n).

Mass of large entries.

Furthermore, let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
be the subset of rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
given by Lemma
6.11
that are hashed to locations without any other rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
. Recall also
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
from the lemma.

We first have that
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1})
since the rows containing entries larger than
\tau_{1}
are perfectly hashed, while rows containing entries between
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{1}
are preserved up to constant factors.

Let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}+\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{>T}
contains the entries of
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
that have absolute value greater than
T
and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}
contains the rest of the entries. Note then that
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{>T}
has at most one nonzero entry per row, and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}
has at most
O(d\cdot r/d\log d)=O(r/\log d)
nonzero entries and thus by Lemma
6.4
,
\lVert\mathbf{B}^{\prime}_{\leqslant T}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant O(r^{1/p})
with probability at least
0.99
. We condition on this event. Then for all
\mathbf{x}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert\lVert\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\mathbf{B}_{>T}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{j}
have disjoint support

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert\sum_{k=\log_{2}\tau_{2}}^{\log_{2}\tau_{1}}2^{k}\Theta(n/2^{kp})-\lVert\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

Lemmas
6.11
and
6.2

\displaystyle=\Omega\left\lparen\frac{r}{d^{2}\log d}(nd^{2}\log d/r)^{1/p}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-O(r)\lVert\mathbf{B}_{\leqslant T}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle=\Omega\left\lparen(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1-1/p}n^{1/p}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-O(r^{1+1/p})\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\Omega((r/d^{2}\log d)^{1-1/p}n^{1/p})\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

Conclusion.

On the other hand, by Lemma
6.14
, the mass of the
O(r/d\log d)
rows that are hashed together with the rows of
\mathbf{A}^{H}
have mass at most

O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log d}}\frac{n^{1/p}}{(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1/p-1}}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=o\left\lparen(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1-1/p}n^{1/p}\right\rparen\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}\geqslant\frac{\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+\lVert\mathbf{A}^{L}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+(r/d^{2}\log d)^{1-1/p}n^{1/p})\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}{O(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{H}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}+n)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}}\geqslant\Omega\left\lparen\left\lparen\frac{(r/d^{2}\log d)}{n}\right\rparen^{1-1/p}\right\rparen.\qed

D.1
Proofs for Section
6.5

Proof of Lemma
6.20
.

For the upper bound, we have by Jensen’s inequality that

\E_{\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}}\lvert\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert\leqslant\sqrt{\E_{\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{D}^{d}}\lvert\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert^{2}}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{2}\E\mathbf{v}_{j}^{2}}=O(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}).

We now focus on the lower bound.

Let
M=O(1)
be the median of
\mathcal{D}
. We define
\mathbf{w}
to be the truncation of
\mathbf{v}
at
M
, that is,
\mathbf{w}_{i}=0
if
\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}\rvert>M
and
\mathbf{w}_{i}=\mathbf{v}_{i}
otherwise. Then by
[
Ver18
, Lemma 6.1.2]
,

\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert\geqslant\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert

so it suffices to bound
\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert
instead.

Note that

\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}E(\mathbf{w}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{w}_{j}\mathbf{x}_{j})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{2}\E\mathbf{w}_{j}^{2}=\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}^{2})

and

\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert^{4}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{4}\E\mathbf{w}_{j}^{4}+3\sum_{j\neq k}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{2}\mathbf{x}_{k}^{2}\E(\mathbf{w}_{j}^{2}\mathbf{w}_{k}^{2})\leqslant O(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{4}^{4}+\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}^{4})=O(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}^{4})

so by the Paley-Zygmund inequality,

\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert\geqslant\sqrt{\lambda}\sqrt{\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert^{2}}\right\rparen=\ProbOp\left\lparen\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert^{2}\geqslant\lambda\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert^{2}\right\rparen\geqslant(1-\lambda)^{2}\frac{(\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert^{2})^{2}}{\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert^{4}}=\Omega(1).

Thus
\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert=\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2})
with constant probability and thus
\E\lvert\langle\mathbf{w},\mathbf{x}\rangle\rvert=\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2})
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.21
.

Let
X\coloneqq\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\rangle
. We have

\ProbOp(\neg\mathcal{E}_{i})=B^{-p}\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{d}

so by the union bound,

\ProbOp(\mathcal{E})\geqslant 1-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\ProbOp(\neg\mathcal{E}_{i})=1-d\ProbOp(\neg\mathcal{E}_{1})=1-\varepsilon.

For
B
large enough, we have by the layer cake theorem that

\E_{Y\sim\mathcal{D}}\left\lparen\lvert Y\rvert\mid\lvert Y\rvert>B\right\rparen\leqslant\frac{1}{\ProbOp(\lvert Y\rvert>B)}\int_{B}^{\infty}O(x^{p})~dx=\frac{1}{\Omega(B^{-p})}O(B^{1-p})=O(B)

since
p\geqslant 2>1
. Then,

\displaystyle\E\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\mid\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{d}\E\left\lparen\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}\rvert\mid\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\E\left\lparen\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}\rvert\mid\neg\mathcal{E}_{i},\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\ProbOp\left\lparen\neg\mathcal{E}_{i}\mid\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen+\E\left\lparen\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}_{i},\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\ProbOp\left\lparen\mathcal{E}_{i}\mid\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{d}O(B)\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}\rvert\frac{1}{d}+O(\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}\rvert)

\displaystyle\leqslant O(B+d)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}.

We then have

\E\lvert X\rvert=\E\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\ProbOp\left\lparen\mathcal{E}\right\rparen+\E\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\mid\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\ProbOp\left\lparen\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\leqslant\E\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen+O(B+d)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\ProbOp\left\lparen\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen.

Since
\E\lvert X\rvert=\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2})=\Omega(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}/\sqrt{d})
by Lemma
6.20
,

O(B+d)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\ProbOp\left\lparen\neg\mathcal{E}\right\rparen=O(B+d)\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}\frac{1}{B^{p}}\leqslant O(\varepsilon)\E\lvert X\rvert

by our choice of
B
. We thus have

\E\lvert X\rvert\leqslant\E\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen+O(\varepsilon)\E\lvert X\rvert

so

\E\left\lparen\lvert X\rvert\mid\mathcal{E}\right\rparen\geqslant(1-O(\varepsilon))\E\lvert X\rvert.\qed
